- [Changed] Say exact-table compatibility in Search and Manage with one passive colored glyph family, including a red mark for a table recorded as not working, and show same-content provenance as a neutral glyph instead of a word.
- [Fixed] Wait for saved cheats to finish applying after a manual start and reread compatibility, so Search and Manage show newly proved evidence without another action.
- [Fixed] Require a usable target process in compatibility evidence, so an unreadable record degrades to no claim instead of failing the whole status read.
- [Fixed] Prove startup compatibility through whichever eligible cheat actually switched on, so a plan with several cheats still records evidence when one of them was already active.
- [Fixed] Refresh compatibility history whenever a manual confirmation may have stored evidence, including a write whose durability could not be proven.
- [Fixed] Treat case-only Windows process spelling changes as the same compatibility and profile target while retaining the displayed spelling.
- [Fixed] Publish confirmed manual compatibility before independent preference writes, preserving new Search and Manage history even when saving preferences fails.
- [Fixed] Require an observed off-to-on startup transition for positive compatibility, including asynchronous activation; already-active cheats provide no new proof.
- [Fixed] Refresh compatibility history after successful autoload so Search and Manage immediately show newly confirmed evidence; advisory refresh failures do not fail autoload.
- [Fixed] Keep blocked-table writes inside one atomic durability boundary so successful clears cannot be reported as pre-commit refusals.
- [Fixed] Compare Steam builds for stopped selected games without scanning historical manifests, and retain neutral success history in device-wide Manage.
- [Fixed] Retain confirmed startup proof across transient storage errors and keep its recurring status payload to one eligible record, independent of autoload size.
- [Fixed] Use and report proven local-call routes for foreground recovery, falling back only on invocation failure and preserving actionable diagnostics.
- [Changed] Use one strict compatibility-evidence schema with no intermediate-format migration, and limit Steam build fingerprint reads to relevant games.
- [Fixed] Reconcile uncertain Failed writes and clears without repeating committed changes; refresh detached Search and Advanced after partial failures and expose corrupt empty failure history for repair.
- [Fixed] Retry late focus exports independently of restore capability, and retain confirmed autoload activations beyond the diagnostic history limit. Value-only and off descendants no longer hide an enabled parent.
- [Fixed] Record confirmed autoload leaf activations, batch compatibility process observation, and recover focus after asynchronous startup or rollback completion. Count delayed foreground confirmations once.
- [Fixed] Preserve Failed independently of success-record write errors, and keep cleared failures from reviving older green marks. Corrupt profiles remain repairable and changed target executables cannot inherit a build match.
- [Fixed] Bind Revoke stops to the expected table and owned session; refreshed Manage confirmations use current holder identities and continue past stale holders.
- [Fixed] Keep cleared failure marks from reviving stale success checks in an open Search, and reject stale Revoke actions before stopping the replacement table.
- [New] Remember confirmed cheat success by game and exact table. Show a green check for matching known build signals, retain neutral history for unknown builds, and require retesting after a build change or cleared failure.
- [Limitation] Controller input recovery after foreground activation still requires confirmation in a real game on SteamOS.
- [Changed] Attempt bounded foreground recovery for a visible window owned by the verified game process after attach, dialog suppression and cheat interaction; include candidate counts, attempts and observed results in support diagnostics.
- [Fixed] Explain exact-content reuse with Same table and remember verified single-table archive identities across providers. A known table failure takes priority over Local; clearing it restores access.
- [Fixed] Keep Review process lookup compact on every screen and clip long information values to one line without extra controller focus stops.
- [Fixed] Revoke detaches the exact selected table and disables its authorization and automatic startup while preserving local bytes and library history. Manage opens without a selected game and can revoke tables held by removed games.
- [Fixed] Invalidate managed frontend state after bulk deletion and reconcile Home immediately, including when the deletion reply is uncertain.

## 0.9.21 — 2026-09-09

- [Fixed] Reconcile consumed setup by its exact completion receipt, including forced reinstall and observer handoff. Let Cancel preempt unavailable status reconciliation, preserve confirmed predecessor completion, and show only the managed setup recovery dialog for setup failures.
- [Fixed] Scope Stop to its own activation and wait for its receipt before reporting success. Retain pending setup mutations without read deadlines, hand terminal setup observation to a newer operation, and preserve the current start refusal instead of a previous failure.
- [Fixed] Make unsuccessful Stop retryable without falsely aborting activation. Keep one managed-setup observer through lost start, poll and cancellation replies, preserve confirmed terminal results, and disable pending Cancel. Show Search-to-Review failures inside Search.
- [Fixed] Keep Stop latched until activation and cancellation both settle, and disable conflicting cheat edits while pinning. Preserve deferred interaction identity, record cancellation lifecycles, and classify resumed setup monitoring as automatic.
- [Changed] Trace panel and modal actions, choices and dismissals with stable action names and safe identities in the bounded support log. Record operation starts and outcomes separately; typing is coalesced without recording entered values.
- [Fixed] Release Manage after synchronous callback failures, guard immediate cancellation and repeated stop requests, and refresh detached removal state after both successful and failed deletion.

## 0.9.20 — 2026-09-08

- [Fixed] Bound Manage holder labels to three shortened names and a remaining-game count. Aggregation is linear in the profile count, display order is deterministic, and deletion remains blocked by every holder even when its name is omitted.
- [Fixed] Keep table imports recoverable across interrupted publication: commit catalogue metadata before blob promotion, preserve shared bytes on metadata failure, and recover validated legacy blobs without metadata at startup without inventing provenance or granting consent.
- [Fixed] Make table deletion, profile/session/launch repair and bulk cleanup commit directory changes durably. Failures after authority moved report an uncertain or partial outcome, and repair refreshes the panel even when durability could not be confirmed.
- [Fixed] Keep missing-file entries and active filters reachable in Manage, restore focus after deletion, and name every game holding the same table. Unavailable entries can be removed or re-imported but cannot be used.
- [Fixed] Fit code rows against every credible viewport height, including displays above the layout breakpoint.
- [Changed] Cut the line under the table list heading to one. It carried three sentences saying that choosing a table associates it with this game and opens the review screen, which is what the next press shows the reader anyway, and it wrapped to three lines above a list whose whole page is counted in rows. What is left is the part that doing it does not tell you: this screen needs neither a source nor a network.
- [Changed] Draw the row that heads the table list as a heading. It was an ordinary field carrying the same weight as the tables under it, so the first thing on the list read as the first table on it. It now has its own ground, an accent and a gap holding the rows off itself, which is the shape this panel already uses for a row that names what follows.
- [Fixed] Ask the page this plugin is drawn in how tall it is, not the context its code runs in. A Decky plugin runs in Steam's shared JavaScript context, whose window is one pixel by one, while its DOM is rendered in the page the user is looking at: 854x534 on a Steam Deck LCD, 1500x844 on a television. Every screen that fitted itself to a height was fitting itself to `1`. The code reading screen showed a single row of code per page because that is what the arithmetic gives for a page one pixel tall, and three attempts at it corrected the arithmetic while the input stayed wrong. A height is now read from a node of the screen asking, which is the only route from one context to the other, and a height too small to be a display is no measurement rather than a very small screen. The review screen was fitting itself the same way: it laid every screen out for a handheld, which was right on a handheld by accident and wrong on a television.
- [Changed] Say which tables are this game's, and which game is on the rest. The two groups are one list on purpose, so the whole screen shares one page budget, and the boundary between them was a few words at the end of one row's label: a page opening in the middle of the device's own tables carried no boundary at all. Rows belonging elsewhere are drawn on their own darker ground now. A table another game has selected also says **in use by** that game on its label, exactly as this game's own says **in use**, and the row says it cannot be deleted while a game is on it: without either, a row that is deliberately protected from removal looked like an ordinary one with a missing button.

## 0.9.19 — 2026-09-08

- [Changed] Describe removal as it is in the lifecycle section too. It still said final deletion needs a future Prepare for removal workflow that restores and verifies owned Steam state: that workflow is not future, it is no longer the only way a table is destroyed now that one can be removed on its own, and there is no owned Steam state to restore because CE Decky never writes any. The same sentence also credited `_uninstall()` with preserving launch ownership, which is a removed feature the profile store now refuses outright. It names the two explicit removal routes, what each is bounded by, and why an update preserves everything.
- [Fixed] Let a deletion that has happened leave the screen at once. The press waited for the read that follows it, which no longer decides whether the deletion succeeded but still decided when the user could see that it had: a read that was merely slow kept the row on screen, held the window's latch and blocked the way out, for a table the backend had already destroyed. The read is no longer awaited; the panel corrects its own list first, and the generation moved with it, so a read in flight cannot put the row back.
- [Fixed] Do not decorate a release that is a date. The label only prefixes a release that is a version number throughout, so a source that releases as 2026-08 keeps what it wrote: treating any leading digit as a number did the same kind of thing to a source's own label that this formatter exists to stop.
- [Fixed] Keep a deletion that happened, and say on the row what went wrong. Removing a table waited for the read that follows it, so a read that failed reported the removal as failed: the row stayed saying the press had not worked, and pressing again met a table that was no longer there. The backend answer is the commit now, the panel corrects its own list before reading again, and a read that fails is recorded as what it is. The screen also asked the list it was opened with rather than what it has left, so deleting the last table left it saying nothing matched a filter nobody had set. And using a table now reports its failure the way removing one does, on the row rather than in a window raised over the screen the press was made on, which can appear behind it and read as a press that did nothing.
- [Security] Check every part of the path a table is removed from, not only its last. Removing a stored table refused a link where the file itself is, and looked no further up: a digest directory that is a link holds an ordinary file, so the leaf is not a link, the file test follows the directory and says yes, and the unlink lands outside this plugin's own tree on something the user owns. The path is walked from the store's root now, with every component required to be a real directory this store owns, and a component that is missing means there is nothing left to remove rather than an error, so a removal that got part way is still finished. A directory where the file belongs is refused outright.
- [Changed] Describe what the code now does, once. The standing design still named a control that had been renamed, said a local import carries no date after the schema that dates one, and endorsed a page budget per list that the screen no longer has; it was also one paragraph carrying four unrelated contracts, and is now four. This version's own entries narrated the route rather than the destination, contradicting each other on the name of the control, on which version leads a row and on where a date comes from, so the superseded ones are collapsed into the outcomes that survived. The store checklist keeps no instruction phrased in a vocabulary that was removed, the operating contract no longer claims the release checker is never invoked directly, and the measurement axiom is stated in the section that owns it rather than twice.
- [New] Manage: one screen for every table this device holds. The panel's table row carries **Search** and **Manage**, because a 300 pixel row that already holds a filename fits two controls. Manage opens with the way to a local file, one line and the only route that works on a device holding nothing, and below it one list with one page budget for the whole screen, one pager and one filter: the table this game has selected first, because that is the one the panel sends the reader here to authorize, then the rest of this game's own, then every other verified table the device holds. Choosing one associates it with the game selected now and opens the ordinary review, where the exact SHA is authorized again. This is the route that needs no provider and no original file, which matters because the association between a game and a table is exactly what deleting the saved setup and discarding an unreadable profile store both throw away while keeping the tables themselves.
- [New] Remove a stored table, and know what that costs. Delete sits beside Use, never takes the focus from it, and confirms by becoming Confirm with the row saying what the second press does rather than raising a window over the window it was pressed in. The wording says what actually happens: permanent removal of a copy that may be the only one this device has, for every game that kept those bytes. A table any game currently has selected is not offered for removal and the row names the game holding it; the backend refuses that case regardless. The bytes go before the record, so a failure leaves a table listed as missing its file rather than storage nothing can name, and a row leaves the screen only when its removal actually succeeded.
- [New] Say which release each row is, and when its bytes arrived. A row leads with the release the provider advertised for those exact bytes, which is what search offered them under and what tells one revision of a table from another: the version inside a `.CT` is the author's own field and is frequently identical across a whole source. Every source is read for one now, including the two that published nothing: a GitHub tree entry from the path it is committed at, a VGTimes row from its name, title and description. The file's own version is the fallback, a release is labelled as its source wrote it, and the date is when the bytes arrived here, from the download that fetched them or from the arrival the table store stamps for a local file.
- [Fixed] Size the code reading screen from what it can measure. Fitting the page reads one number and estimated the other, and the last change made the estimate outrank the measurement: where the derived chrome was too large for the screen the helper decided the display could not really be that small and drew past its edge on purpose, which is the failure the fitting exists to prevent. The uncertain number is the chrome, so the screen measures it: the difference between the whole sheet and the block of code inside it is the same quantity without the guesswork, it settles after one pass, and the derived figure remains the first answer because nothing can be measured before something is drawn. The measurement decides again at every height, never returns a page of no rows, and never knowingly draws more than the display holds.
- [Fixed] Stop showing a release as vv2, and stop describing one row from two downloads. GitHub advertises its tag exactly as the repository wrote it, so a table tagged v2 was shown as vv2 on the search rows, in Manage and under Advanced alike: all three put their own v in front of whatever the source published. One formatter now, which prefixes a bare number, leaves a tag that already carries a marker alone, and leaves a release that is a name rather than a number entirely alone. It formats what is stored, not only what is ingested, because origins written before it are on devices now. Manage also composed a row from whichever origin happened to have a version and the provider and date of the newest one, so it could pair a release from the source a table came from once with the name and date of the one it came from last; the row describes a single download.
- [Fixed] Make Manage one screen rather than two lists that add up. Each section paged six rows of its own, so a game with tables of its own and a device holding more put twelve of them on a screen that fits one page, which is the reachability the paging was added to protect. The two are one ordered sequence now, with one page budget of its own rather than one borrowed from the search results: the table this game is on comes first, because the panel sends the reader here to authorize exactly that one and sorting by filename could put it pages away, then the rest of this game's own, then what the device holds for other games. A deleted row leaves the open window, which could not be re-propped from the panel and went on offering presses against bytes that were gone; a deletion that failed keeps its row and says why on the screen the press was made on, rather than in a window raised over it. Delete is not offered for a table any game has selected, because the backend refuses exactly those, and the row names the game holding it. An armed confirmation is disarmed by paging or filtering, since two presses only mean anything while they are one interaction. And the confirmation says what actually happens: permanent removal of the only copy this device may have, for every game that kept those bytes.
- [Fixed] Write the schema a metadata record actually is. Adding a provider origin to a table stamped its record as the schema before last while writing a field only the current one has, because the marker was written from three places and one of them had not moved. The reader tolerates it, which is what made it a trap rather than a symptom: nothing looks wrong until something migrates on the marker. One constant now, used by every route that writes a record.
- [Fixed] Remove a table in the order the failures decide. Deleting one removed its record and then its bytes, so a failure between the two left the state nothing can recover from: the row vanished from every list while the file stayed on the disk, a retry answered that there is no such table, and no route in Game Mode could name or remove what was left. The bytes go first and the record last now, each step repeats safely so a press that failed part way is finished by pressing again, and a link that found its way into the store is unlinked as the entry it is rather than followed to a file the user owns somewhere else.
- [Fixed] Let a table whose authorization was withdrawn be authorized again. Selected and authorized are two facts and the picker used only the first, so Use was disabled for the selected table on the ground that there was nothing left to do to it. Withdrawing the authorization leaves that table selected and unusable, and the way back is opening it once through the review screen, which is exactly what Use does: the one press that fixes the state was the one press the state disabled, while the panel was sending the user to make it. The row says it needs authorizing, and the panel now names Manage rather than two controls that no longer sit where it said.
- [New] Date a table that came from a file, not only one that was downloaded. A download records when it was fetched in the origin its acquisition writes, and a table opened from a local file has no origin at all, so the screen listing what is stored could say when half of it arrived. Table metadata now records when bytes first arrived on this device, written by the import that first stores them and carried forward by any later import of the same file, because a second copy of one table is not a second arrival. A table imported before this was tracked claims no date rather than borrowing the file's timestamp, which says when the bytes were written and not when the user chose them.
- [Changed] Say what is unsettled in one place, and stop describing a tracker that is gone. `docs/DECKY_STORE.md` is routed to by the top-level contract and described a release state from an audited snapshot: a blocker held open by two identifiers that no longer exist, a registry in a script that was deleted, and the same plan restated in five sections. Release state is now read where it actually lives, which is `Still worth checking` in `docs/FIELD_NOTES.md` and the checker that reads it, and the dated audit is labelled as the provenance it is. The deleted target-gate vocabulary is gone from the standing documents and from one release diagnostic that still spoke it, and `AGENTS.md` is materially shorter: its cleanup section is the rules that are still live rather than a narration of what an earlier one did, measurement discipline moved to the document that owns measurement, and the install and CI-snapshot rules keep their guarantees and hand their mechanism to the helper row and the workflow beside them.
- [Fixed] Keep the device's stored tables out of one game's search results. A search row is an offer to use that table for the game being searched for, and handing the whole device to that screen turned every table stored for some other game into a standalone result for this one. Search offers rows from this game's own tables again; the device-wide list reaches it only as an exact-digest lookup, so a provider row advertising bytes already here is still answered from the device rather than downloaded again, and the rest are reached through Stored, by a press that says which game they are being taken for.
- [New] Reach every table on the device without a provider and without the original file. The way back to a stored table went entirely through the association between a game and that table, and the association is exactly what the two supported recoveries discard: deleting the saved setup keeps the tables and removes the state beside them, and discarding an unreadable profile store starts an empty one. Both say the tables are kept, and afterwards nothing in Game Mode could name them, because search needs a provider row that maps to those bytes and Local file needs a file the user may no longer have. A game with no library of its own now offers Stored, listing what the device holds; a game with one keeps Imported and gains a second section for the rest. Choosing one is explicit and guesses nothing about which game it used to belong to: it associates the table with the game selected now and opens the ordinary review screen, where the exact SHA is authorized again.
- [Fixed] Stop an unreadable download status from cancelling the download. Retrying a refused status read was half the fix: once the retry budget ran out the window still wrote failed into the acquisition, and that is not a label but an action. A terminal status turns the only press here into Close, Close cancels what it closes, and a transfer running perfectly well behind several refused reads was cancelled because this window had stopped being able to look at it. None of those failures says anything about the transfer, so none of them touches its state now. The last thing the backend actually reported stays, the window says it cannot read the progress and that the download has not been stopped, and Retry status looks again. Only the backend, or its authoritative answer that it has no such acquisition, can make one terminal.
- [Changed] Take the finished milestones out of the operating contract. `AGENTS.md` still routed by identifiers for a tracker that no longer exists: an invariant deferred in-process table swap until a gate nobody can look up, repository-only work was to be filed under another, and the pre-publication checklist listed five documents and the production source as still carrying gate identifiers when every one of them had been cleaned already. A checklist that lists finished work cannot be told from one that lists work nobody has done. The two identifiers left in production comments are gone with it, the deferred capability now says the evidence that would settle it, and the checklist holds only what is still owed.
- [Fixed] Say when the look for the game's processes failed. The review screen is where the user picks the exact executable Cheat Engine will attach to, and the press that refills that list swallowed its own failure: the spinner stopped, the previous choices stayed, and nothing said the look had not happened, so a stale list was presented as a fresh one at the moment it matters most. The failure is now said beside the choice it affects, the list says it is the last answer that worked, and a look that works clears it.
- [Fixed] Stop a refused status read from aborting a download that is still running. The window that owns every provider download turned any rejected poll into a terminal failure and stopped polling, and closing it cancels whatever is not terminal, so one refused RPC became the abort of a healthy transfer. A read that fails says nothing about the transfer behind it: those are retried now, widening, and only a run of them gives up. The one answer that is terminal on arrival is the backend saying it no longer has that acquisition, and that answer also used to trap the user: Close cancels what it closes, cancelling something the backend has dropped raises the same error, and both presses this window has went down that path. It knows there is nothing left to cancel and closes.
- [Fixed] Do not accept "Stop using it" and then drop it. The question about a refused table is put by a wait that is allowed to give up on the panel latch, because a latch that never comes free must not swallow the question; that leaves the press arriving while an operation still holds it, and the answer makes five durable writes that refuse to run beside another operation. The dialog had already closed itself, so the answer was accepted, discarded, and reported as an operation the user never started, over a table that went on applying. The answer waits for the latch the same way the question did, and where it never comes free it says the table was not stopped and that it can be answered again, which is about the table rather than about the machinery.
- [Fixed] Stop ending a session during the handoff it is supposed to wait out. Both supervisors read the disappearance of the PIDs observed when the launch was prepared as proof the game had gone, which is exactly the state the scan scheduler beside them calls an ordinary launcher handoff and keeps scanning through: the launcher and the bootstrap retire, and the game arrives after them under PIDs no frozen set can contain. A session prepared before the game had started its own executable therefore had its Cheat Engine stopped the moment the launcher exited, seconds before the target it was waiting for appeared. The proof is now the game itself being gone, which is a complete walk of the process table finding nothing that reports this AppID, and a target that was seen alive and is not there now. Both supervisors decide by one function rather than by two copies of an expression that had already drifted apart.
- [Changed] Say when to reach for each development helper, not only what it does. Every row of the reusable target tools table in `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md` now opens with the situation that sends an agent looking for it, ahead of the mechanism it already described: a row that says it correlates bounded plugin state does not match somebody holding the question of what table a game has selected. `scripts/target_snapshot.py` gained the module docstring it never had and a `--help` that describes it rather than running the capture, which is what the survey route promises for every helper under `scripts/`, and the two generated tracked assets have a home beside the screenshots they are cut from.
- [Changed] Route an agent to the helper that already answers its question. The tracked helpers were listed in `AGENTS.md` by what each one is, under a heading that is a condition about the machine, so a run looking for what a game currently has selected read past the row that prints it and spent six calls and a two-minute `find` re-deriving it by hand. The list is now its own section, ahead of the conditional one, its rows are the questions rather than the capabilities, and it says that a `cat`, `grep`, `find` or `python3 -c` about to be written over plugin state is the shape of a helper nobody looked for. `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md` stays the authority for what each helper guarantees, because a copy of that in a document which outranks it would win while being wrong.
- [Fixed] Let a game take back the table it was just told to stop using. Answering "This table did not work" with Stop using it clears the selection and records that table as the one this game came from, so it can be chosen again without hunting for it. Clearing the mark under Advanced and loading the same file is then the ordinary way back, and it was refused: the pointer named the table being selected, which is not a previous selection, and the write was rejected on its way to disk. The user was told their own downloaded table could not be selected, with no network and nothing else to select instead. Selecting the table a game would go back to now simply leaves it with nothing to go back to.
- [Fixed] Say how long starting Cheat Engine actually takes. Both screens that describe the wait called it a few minutes on a handheld, which is what the backend deadline allows rather than what a Steam Deck does: it is usually within fifteen seconds. A wait described as ten times its own length reads as a plugin that has stopped answering, and the user reaching for Cancel CE launch is the plugin talking them into it.
- [Changed] Put the ring on Search once a table has been confirmed as one that did not work. The answer that retires a table is given from a window that has usually taken the quick access panel with it, and the panel Steam builds when the user looks again opens where it always opens, on Advanced. That is not what somebody who has just lost their table is going to press: they are going to look for another one. The request outlives the panel and is taken by whichever one can act on it, whether the question came from Configure cheats, from a pinned cheat on the panel itself, or from an auto-load that failed, and it is retired the moment the ring lands so that one answer moves it once.
- [Fixed] Take a live-read failure off the panel once there is no table it could be about, and call the review screen's process refresh Refresh. The message from a failed hydration was replaced by the next failure and never by a success, and nothing re-hydrates a game whose table was taken away under it, so stopping a table left Live cheat state could not be refreshed on a panel with no table and no session to refresh it from.
- [Fixed] Take a stopped table off a panel that was not there when it was stopped. The answer closes Configure cheats, which takes the quick access panel with it, and then makes five durable writes with the selection cleared last. Steam builds a new panel when the user looks again and that panel reads the authority once on its way up, racing a chain of writes it knows nothing about; it won, so a table the user had just stopped using was still on the panel with its Load and Configure rows while the device had already forgotten it. An answer that outlives the panel says when it has finished now, and whichever panel is alive reads again. The timed catch-up stays for what it was measured against, a single write landing a moment late.
- [Fixed] Ask about a refused table even when closing Configure cheats took the panel with it. The question is a window the panel opens after closing another one, and closing that one is itself a way for the panel to go, so a rule that dropped the question once the panel had gone dropped it into the gap it had just made: the notification arrived, the window did not, and the table went on applying. The window is Steam's rather than the panel's and outlives it, and the answer already re-checks the selected game against the one it was asked about.
- [Fixed] Put the question about a refused table even when the latch it waits for is never released. It waits because two of the three callers raise it from inside their own action, which owns the panel's latch until that action returns, and an answer given while it is held is refused. Waiting without a bound meant a latch that never came free took the question with it: the notification appeared, the window did not, and the table went on applying. The wait is bounded now, and every branch that ends in not asking records which one it was, because working that out cost two rounds on the device and should cost a log line.
- [Fixed] Close Configure cheats and put the question, when Cheat Engine refuses one of that table's cheats. The question opens as a window of its own, and it was raised while the picker was still up: a window behind a window, which on the device is a Steam notification saying the table did not work and nothing else, with Apply available again and no way to reach the one answer that helps. The picker closes first now. A table that ran a cheat and had it come straight back off is not a thing to go on configuring.
- [Fixed] Put the unapplied-changes prompt where the press that opens it is. Cancel in Configure cheats opens a prompt rather than closing over edits that are not applied, and that prompt was a row near the top of the list, which on any table worth paging through is off the screen from the footer. The press did something and looked as though it had done nothing, so Cancel and the controller's Back both read as dead. It is beside the buttons now, the same place the Apply failure above it already uses.
- [Fixed] Ask about a table that did not work, however many times Apply is pressed. The guard that keeps one question to one table was taken when it was decided to ask rather than when the question was actually put, several awaits earlier: anything that stopped the wait in between left the table recorded as asked about with nothing having been asked, and every later Apply returned at that guard. A table that refused every cheat could be applied for as long as the user cared to press, and the one question that would have retired it was never put again. The guard is taken when the window opens and released if it cannot.
- [Fixed] Put the ring back on Look inside when the reader returns from the table's code. Reading the table replaces the review screen rather than opening over it, so coming back mounts that screen again and Steam's focus lands where it would on a screen just opened. The press the reader is in the middle of is the one that opened the code, so the ring goes back on it, which is what the Advanced screen already did for the sub-screens it opens.
- [Fixed] Count the cheats that are on, not the machinery under them. A table's enclosing scripts and its attach-only record are switched on by CE Decky to reach the cheat that needs them, and the picker lists them apart from the cheats for that reason, but the panel added them in: one switch on, and the line above it read 2 active. It now reads 1 active (1 script), and the names listed under it are cheats the user chose.
- [Fixed] Say that Cheat Engine is starting, once, and hold still while it does. A launch takes its own time on a handheld, and for all of it the panel showed Cannot start yet above the reason Cheat Engine is already running for this game, which describes the user's own launch back to them as the obstacle, while the runtime line above it cycled through three descriptions of the same not-yet as the poll walked the session through them. The runtime line now says the launch is running, with a spinner and the way to stop it, and nothing else on the panel talks about the wait. The review screen's own progress row was rebuilt and re-wrapped on every step for the same reason, the step sharing one string with the counter and with the sentence explaining the wait: the step has the label to itself now, the explanation is said once, and the counter sits in a column where a digit changing cannot reflow the row.
- [Fixed] Stop a focused pinned cheat twitching. The scroll that shows the end of a name too long for the row was expressed as a pair of percentages, which is the overflow when there is one and zero when there is not and needs no measurement, but is also two values resolved against two boxes: the endpoint moves whenever either width does, including when the rule takes the line's own width clamp off at the moment the animation starts. Both lines of a focused pinned cheat twitched, including on rows whose text fits and has nothing to reveal. The overflow is measured now and the scroll travels that one length, so a name that is cut still scrolls to show its end and a name the reader can already finish carries no animation at all.
- [Changed] Tighten the review screen where a handheld needs it and where it read as one block too few. Every section heading now keeps a gap under it, so the first row of a list is not drawn against the heading's own rule. Look inside this table moves beside the sentence that asks the question it answers, at the size of a row control rather than a full-width button above it; where a table carries no executable content there is no such sentence and it keeps its row. On a short screen the press that looks for the game's processes again is in one group with the control it fills, so the stick moves across to it rather than down, and it is centred against that whole block.
- [Changed] Fit the review screen to a handheld, and give every modal's actions one row the stick moves across. The two buttons at the bottom of a modal sat side by side and behaved as though they were stacked, because two siblings in a plain box are two separate steps to Steam; they are one horizontal group now, and sized by their own text rather than by the width of the dialog, which is the size the search screen's own footer control already uses. On a short screen the review screen also puts its four lines of identity two to a line and moves the press that looks for the game's processes again alongside the control it fills, instead of a full-width row under a control that already has the width. A display with the room keeps the rows it had.
- [Changed] Show every table download in a window of its own. Pressing a result started the download where the list was: the screen went inert and what was happening appeared in a row under the results, which for most of a list is several screens below the press, so the panel looked as though it had stopped answering. Only the two sources that make the user wait out their own countdown had a window; every other download had none. Now each one opens the same compact window, with the file, what the download is doing, its counter and a spinner in one block, and a Cancel that stops it. An import hands straight over to the review screen, and a failure says what happened and returns to the list on a press.
- [Fixed] Stop cutting the text of a row that says it is read where it stands. Home's first Cheats row had both at once: the ring rested on a line of pure state, and the end of that line was hidden behind a press, because cutting a line to the row is exactly what makes a row worth reaching. In a 300 pixel panel "Cheat Engine is not running for this table. Details are under Advanced." is cut by 93 pixels, on a television as much as on a handheld. Such a row wraps now, with the rule that also breaks a process name or a path rather than letting it run past the edge, and the ring passes over it. The two runtime lines that carry a message from Cheat Engine or the backend keep their stop and open on a press, because that text has no length this panel knows and wrapping all of it would push the panel's own controls off the screen.
- [Fixed] Fit the code reading screen to the display it is on. A handheld gives that screen 534 CSS pixels of height where a television gives 844, and a page of twenty-six rows plus the screen's own heading, section row, pager and button does not fit the first: the sheet was drawn from 115 pixels above the top of the display, so its heading, the row naming the section and the first lines of every page were off the screen with nothing that could reach them, and the ring opened at the bottom because the bottom was all that was on the display. The page is now as many rows as the screen actually holds, reserving the height of each note the section carries, and never more than the twenty-six a full-size screen already showed, so nothing changes on a display that had the room.
- [New] Capture the screen of a SteamOS device across the network. `scripts/target_screenshot.py --remote USER@HOST` runs the same capture on that device and keeps the frame here, so a handheld's screen can be looked at from the machine the work is being done on without a checkout at the far end. Every capture is now named after the machine it came from and the label the caller gave it, in that order.

## 0.9.18 — 2026-09-07

- [Fixed] Report the processes a truncated scan had already skipped. The two paths that stop the walk early, reaching the bounded scan limit and reaching the bounded matching-PID limit, built their observation without the set of processes already skipped, so the published diagnostic read zero unattributable processes for a scan that had encountered a dozen. It cannot bring the liveness defect back, because a truncated scan reports unknown and never absent, but it made the number wrong in the direction that matters: a report is told the opposite of what happened. It also falsified the claim made when that count was introduced, that the published value is what the walk actually saw. Both paths carry it now.
- [Fixed] Remember every process the scan could not attribute, not the first thousand of them. The protection against proving an absence by declining to look kept a bounded set of skipped processes, and that bound was tighter than the walk's own: past it, later skipped processes were discarded silently, so a live target with an unreadable environment and a high PID was skipped again and reported gone again, in exactly the windows with no cached identity to catch it. The published diagnostic was wrong in the same case, reporting the size of the cap as the number of unattributable processes. The set is now bounded only by the walk, which already refuses to report an absence at all once it reaches its own limit, so the set is complete for every observation that is allowed to prove one and the published count is the real one.
- [Fixed] Keep the whole failure stack the panel log promised to keep. A stack was bounded to its own generous limit and then passed through the ordinary field sanitiser, which cut every value to a fraction of that, so almost all of the evidence a frontend defect leaves was discarded on the way into the ring buffer while a comment beside it said the stack was the one field allowed past that bound. Sanitising takes the bound as an argument now and the stack field gets its own, with the same control-character and whitespace handling as every other value.
- [Fixed] Close the identity check against a PID changing owner mid-check. Confirming the target reads its start time, then its command line, then its environment, and a PID is a name that can be handed to another process between any two of those. An old start time could therefore be combined with a replacement process's executable and declared app and read as the same process. The start time is read again at the end and must still match, which is what proves one process answered all of it. Resolving a fresh identity had the same shape, taking the PID from an observation and never revalidating what it declares, so it now confirms the app and re-reads the start time before returning.
- [Fixed] Stop the process-table scan proving an absence by declining to look. The scan decides what a process belongs to by reading its environment, and procfs refuses that for a same-user non-dumpable process, so a live target in that state was skipped and reported gone. The previous release protected only the case where the session already held a confirmed identity, which leaves two ordinary windows unprotected: a fresh session before its first acquisition, and recovered supervision after a reload, which restores the durable record that the target was seen and starts with no identity at all. The second can stop the owned Cheat Engine immediately. The observation now remembers the processes it could not attribute, and an answer that would be absent is checked against them first: a process running the exact target executable that will not say which app it belongs to makes the state unknown, which never ends a launch. It is narrow on purpose, because most of an ordinary machine's process table is unreadable to a normal user and treating that as ambiguity would mean a session could never notice a game exiting at all. The count of unattributable processes is published as a diagnostic; the list of PIDs stays internal.
- [Fixed] Write the README's cost figures for someone who did not watch the project being built. The paragraph read like a changelog entry: it said what the plugin's share used to be, which two changes reduced it and in which version, and carried an older reading kept for comparison. A reader who has never seen this repository needs what a session costs now, on what hardware, and what to do about it. It states the two measured configurations and the one thing that is actionable, that a device with nothing to spare pays in frames, and nothing about how the number got there. The Decky Loader requirement was development history as well, and stale: it named the version this was first developed against rather than the one a user needs, and a loader older than 3.2.8 does not appear in the Quick Access menu on current Steam at all, so nothing installed through it is reachable.
- [Changed] Settle the backend-cost question and delete the document that held it, moving everything it established into the durable contracts first. What a live session costs is now measured on both machines and attributed to the loop that spends it, so a temporary document describing an open question would only describe finished work. Its measurements, the device conclusions behind them and the fact that the backend costs nothing measurable without a session went to `docs/FIELD_NOTES.md`; the loops that run during a session, the cheap target confirmation and its fallback rules, and the three deferred capability-path reductions went to `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md`; what a poll rate is actually paid for in, and why neither reduction traded latency, went to `docs/DESIGN.md`; the three-window measurement procedure, the two rules that keep the counters from destroying their own reading, and what the compositor pipe cannot answer went to `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md`; and the cost a user can feel went to `README.md`, where the figures were materially out of date. Two conclusions are removed rather than migrated, and are named here so they do not disappear with the file: that the backend is the largest single part of a session on a handheld, which was true and is not, and the estimate of what an interval change might recover, which was superseded by measuring it.
- [Fixed] Stop recording a closed cheat picker as a failed query. That query owns an abort controller and its cleanup aborts it, so the runtime client throws between batches, and the new logging recorded it before checking why. Closing the picker mid-query is the user doing what the control is for, and the same release had already said aborted queries stay unlogged, which this contradicted. The aborted case returns without logging, as the panel's own boundaries already did, and a real initial-query failure is still recorded.
- [Fixed] Lower the running-game detector's failure latch where recovery actually happens. The clear sat below the early returns that every ordinary outcome takes, one game running or a remembered selection restored, so after one logged failure it was never reached: no recovery was recorded and, far worse, the latch stayed raised and silenced every later failure. A latch that never clears is worse than no latch at all, because it turns one logged failure into silence about all the rest. It is cleared as soon as the observation has been taken and the stale-action guards have passed, which is the whole of what recovery means, and a regression drives failure, recovery and a second failure through the one-game path.
- [Fixed] Stop the target log from misstating the fact the decision turns on. Both supervision loops wrote their line before updating target_seen, and the line was deduplicated on state and PID alone, so the first sight of a target was recorded as target_seen=false and the moment it became true produced no line at all. The flag is now part of what counts as a change, and the line is written after that fact has settled, so a first sighting reads as what it is.
- [Fixed] Say why managed-data deletion refused, and record that it ran at all. The ownership probe fails closed on an exception, which is right, but it discarded the exception, so a probe that could not answer and a Cheat Engine that really is running produced the same sentence about the user's machine and wanted completely different responses. The exception is recorded before the refusal it becomes. Deletion also owns its own worker boundary rather than going through the operation registry, so it inherited none of that boundary's started and failed records: the only destructive action this plugin offers now keeps its own, with a refusal recorded as the completed decision it is and an exception recorded as what nothing downstream could reconstruct.
- [Fixed] Latch the running-game detector's failures instead of leaving them unrecorded. Parts of that pipeline are entirely Steam-side, so when they fail no backend record exists to compensate and the panel was the only place that knew. It runs every three seconds, so a line per tick would bury the ring buffer it writes into: the first failure is logged once and the recovery is logged once.
- [Fixed] Record the runtime and catalog failures only the panel ever sees. Several boundaries caught an error, degraded the interface and wrote nothing to the log the support bundle carries, and the ones that matter most are the failures the backend cannot know about: a session or acknowledgement identity that does not match, a bridge result that says not ok, a read-back that disagrees. All of those are produced after the backend answered every call successfully, so a bundle could show a healthy backend beside a user reporting stale cheats. Hydration, the live snapshot sweep, the cheat modal's initial query, its reconciliation after a failed Apply and Apply itself now each write one line at their own boundary, as do the catalog's action runner, the acquisition modal's terminal poll, complete and cancel, its best-effort cancellation at unmount, which can otherwise leave acquisition work running with nothing on screen that could stop it, and managed-setup cancellation, which unlike setup start does not pass through the panel's action runner. Aborted queries stay unlogged, because a panel the user closed is not a failure.
- [Fixed] Record what the supervision loops decide, which until now they did silently. Two questions a bug report asks are answered on this path or nowhere: Cheat Engine outliving a game the user quit, and Cheat Engine stopping while they were still playing. Both turn on a target identity and a baseline state that left no trace, and a recovered session stopping owned Cheat Engine had no record of the decision at all, only of its effect. Each loop now writes one line when the launch-time process set appears or disappears, one when what it believes about its target moves, and one when a recovered session decides to stop, all of them on the change rather than on the tick, because these questions are asked every second and a line per answer would add work at the rate the answer is measured at and flush the ring buffer the support bundle carries. The ambiguous case is named as itself: a target still at its PID with its start time and executable intact, whose environment procfs will not hand over, is reported as unreadable rather than as an ordinary unknown, because that is precisely the state a user experiences as Cheat Engine refusing to stop. Identifiers are truncated the way the rest of this log truncates them.
- [Fixed] Never call an unreadable target gone. The cheap confirmation correctly refused a target whose environment procfs would not hand over, but the complete scan it fell through to skips exactly those processes, so a live game whose environment became unreadable could be reported absent and stop the owned Cheat Engine attached to it. That contradicts the invariant this project already recorded, that an unreadable same-user process is ambiguous rather than gone. The confirmation now answers unreadable separately from changed, and an absence reported after that answer becomes unknown, which never ends a launch, with the identity kept so the next tick confirms it the moment procfs answers again. A target that really exited, and a different process occupying the same PID, are still absent: unreadable is the only ambiguity this widens.
- [Fixed] Walk the process table once when the target has to be found, not twice. Acquiring the identity asked for the target's state and then took a second observation to name the process behind it, so the most expensive path this backend has ran twice on every acquisition and on every failed confirmation that resolved back to present. Only the first of the two was counted as a scan, which understated exactly the path the identity exists to avoid. One observation now feeds both answers, and regressions assert one walk and one counted scan for a first acquisition and for a fallback that finds the target again.
- [New] Confirm the target the session already saw instead of walking the process table for it. The scan is now 85% to 88% of what this backend spends while a session is live, and almost all of that is reading an environment for every process on the machine to find one. Once a complete scan has named the target, the session holds four facts about it, its PID, its start time, the Windows executable it declares and the AppID the process itself declares, and every tick after that reads those three files of that one process instead. None of the four is decoration: a PID is reused, start time defeats reuse, and start time with an executable still proves only that something of that name is alive at that PID, so the declared AppID is what ties the process back to this session's game. The boundary that makes it safe is one sentence, and it is a test rather than a comment: the cheap check may only ever answer present. A start time that moved, an executable that changed, an environment that cannot be read, a PID that is gone, any of them falls through to the complete scan, and nothing may be called absent without one, because only the complete table can prove a process is not in it. An identity is dropped the moment a scan stops confirming it, so a stale one can never be offered back. The confirmation is counted as its own path, so what it costs and how often it falls through are both readable.

## 0.9.17 — 2026-09-07

- [New] Confirm the baseline-check fix on both machines, one workload each, and close the attribution. The backend fell from 3.33% of a core to 1.59 on the Steam Machine and from 9.21% to 4.33 on the handheld, 52% and 53%, and the session as a whole from 9.6 to 7.9 and from 22.5 to 17.8. The check now runs 0.99 and 0.95 times a second, one call per tick, against the 21 a second its cost implied before. Per-call costs did not move, 102.7 ms against 105.5 for the process-table walk and 2.42 against 2.63 for the check itself, so the whole saving is in how many calls are made rather than in what one costs, which is what the diagnosis said it would be. The unattributed share went from 61% to 3% and 2%, so what the backend spends while a session is live is now named. Nothing polls less often than it did, no interval changed and no latency was traded. The prediction recorded before the run, 1.4 and 3.7 points, came in at 1.59 and 4.33: right in direction and order and low in magnitude, because it costed the one remaining call at the bare bench figure rather than at what the counted path spends.
- [Fixed] Stop reporting malformed version data as two versions disagreeing. The install authority compared the backend's reported version and the installed package's version against Decky's inventory without first checking that either was a usable version string, so an absent, null or non-string value produced the message about a loader still catching up. That explanation cannot apply to identity data that is missing or malformed, and following it means waiting for something that will never resolve. Each side is validated before it is compared, and a version that is not a usable string is reported as that, with what was actually found. The comparison itself and its fail-closed refusal are unchanged.
- [Fixed] Keep the running game inside the state probe's bounded profile list. Profiles are sorted by name and the report took the first of them up to a cap, so a device holding more profiles than the cap could report an AppID as running while omitting that AppID's own profile purely because its name sorts late, which is exactly the second call the field exists to remove. The running games lead the list now and the rest fill what is left in the ordinary order, nothing is reported twice, the cap is unchanged, and an unreadable profile store still reports no profiles with the reason rather than an empty list that would read as a device with none.
- [Changed] Extend the measurement discipline to what is said, not only to what is recorded. The rule that a conclusion states what was observed rather than what was inferred sat inside the paragraph about writing durable notes, so a diagnostic that was never going to be recorded got none of it: this run asserted a network bottleneck from one nested transfer and then an internet link speed from one single-stream download, and both were wrong for the same reason, that a single sample bounds a quantity from one side only. A transfer or a download measures a floor and never a ceiling, because the far end, the route and one connection each cap it independently. The contract now says the discipline covers what is asserted to the maintainer as well as what is written down, and names that asymmetry so the next reading says which side it bounds.
- [Fixed] Say which two versions disagree when the install authority refuses. Reading the authority immediately after an install reported that live plugin metadata disagrees with Decky inventory, which is what a machine holding a plugin this run must not authorize also reports, and the message named neither version. The ordinary cause is a few seconds of the loader catching up with an install that already landed, and the alarming one is a real identity conflict; the reader was sent to the second first. Both refusals now name what each side reports and say that the difference is expected immediately after an install and is a conflict if it survives a second read. The refusal itself is unchanged and there is no retry: waiting quietly for the numbers to agree is how a real conflict becomes invisible. A backend that answered with no status at all is reported as that rather than as a disagreement.
- [Changed] Let the state probe answer the question that reaches it. It correlated state by AppID and reported a bare profile count, so the two things a setup actually needs, which AppID is running and what each profile selects, were the two things it could not say: reaching it meant finding the AppID with an ad-hoc snippet against the production module and then reading the profile store by hand, which happened once per machine during the cross-machine measurement. It now reports the running AppIDs, with the reason when the process table cannot be read, and a bounded summary for every profile rather than a count, the same summary it already produced for a single game. Six calls become one.
- [Fixed] Stop a permanently gone baseline from cancelling the target scan's interval for the rest of a session. A gone baseline is a reason to scan now rather than at the next interval, and that gate never said how long the reason lasts. A launch prepared while the game is still bootstrapping records the launcher's PIDs as its baseline and Steam's handoff retires every one of them, so the condition became true and stayed true through an entirely ordinary session, and the most expensive thing this backend does ran every second instead of every three for all of it. A gone baseline is now urgent while it is news and while the target has never been positively seen, which is the convergence the gate was written for; once the target has been seen, a baseline that is merely still gone returns to the ordinary interval. Nothing about what may end a launch changes: only a complete scan returning absent may, and this decides only when a scan happens.
- [Fixed] Walk the baseline once, and take it as a collection rather than an iterable. The ordering hint built a set from the baseline to test membership and then iterated the baseline again, which a one-shot generator does not survive: the first read would exhaust it, the second would see an empty baseline, and an empty baseline reads as the game being gone. Every production caller passes a tuple or a frozenset, so nothing failed, but a liveness check must not be the kind of function where that could. The hint is now tested directly and the baseline traversed once, skipping the PID already tried, which also removes a set and a list allocated on every tick.
- [Fixed] Keep the closing counter read out of every paired local reading. The backend counters are read over a socket with a five-second timeout, and that read sat between the process sample that ends the window and the system, pressure and per-process reads that follow it. So an unreachable or slow Decky landed its wait inside the system CPU numerator, the pressure delta and the per-process deltas, while the window those are divided by knew nothing about it: system busy could be overstated materially and the pressure and detail spans covered different seconds from the window the report declares. Every closing local reading is now taken in the same order as its opening counterpart and before the counter read, so each pair spans the window itself, and the counter read is last because it is the one that can take seconds.
- [Fixed] Stop describing the counter interval as the window it brackets, and take the window between the samples. The backend's counters cannot be read inside the window they describe, so their interval necessarily surrounds it, and the closing read was taken after the pressure and per-process detail reads rather than beside the sample that ends the window. The report now takes each anchor at its process sample, reads the closing counters immediately after the second one, and states how far outside each end the counter interval sits, so backend path percentages and process percentages are never compared as though they covered the same seconds. The same change fixes a smaller error in the other direction: the elapsed time was measured after the trailing procfs reads, putting their duration into the denominator of every CPU percentage and understating all of them by however long one pass took.
- [Fixed] Stop counting a frame reading whose focus never arrived. The compositor writes the rate first and the focus it belongs to a couple of milliseconds later, so a window ending between the two leaves the last reading unpaired. Unpaired readings were counted in the mean and the spread and were not counted as partial, which contradicts the pairing the rest of this code rests on and lets a focus change through at the one boundary nothing else catches. A reading without its focus is now partial, like the first reading of a window and the first after a focus change. If a compositor ever stopped writing focus at all, every reading would become unusable and the report would say so through its sample counts rather than quietly averaging readings it cannot place.
- [Fixed] Give the baseline short-circuit an order to work with. Stopping at the first live PID only helps if a live one comes early, and nothing said it would: the live supervisor holds its baseline as a set, where the order is arbitrary, and the recovered supervisor holds it sorted by PID, where the low numbers are the launcher and bootstrap processes Steam's handoff retires first. Either way the survivor can sit at the end and the tick reads everything before it, once a second, to reach a PID that answered the same way a second ago. Each loop now keeps whichever PID last proved the game alive and offers it back as the first candidate. It is an ordering hint and nothing else: a PID is tried only while it is still one of this session's own baseline PIDs, the predicate is order-independent, and a hint that has stopped proving anything falls through to the ordinary traversal. Proving the game gone stays exhaustive and an unreadable PID still counts as alive. A regression covers the production shape the plain short-circuit does not help with, twenty dead PIDs ahead of one survivor, and holds every hint against the unhinted answer.
- [Changed] Re-rank the optimization candidates against what was actually measured, and correct one this document got wrong. Three of the five are about the capability path, which does not run while the panel is hidden, so none of them recovers anything in the state a session is spent in; they stay worth doing for panel responsiveness and are not the lever. The fifth predicted that the one-second baseline check is not what costs, on the grounds that a single call is under a millisecond, and that reasoned about the call rather than about how many of them a tick makes: twenty a second was the missing 61%. The correction is recorded rather than quietly replaced. What is left is the fourth, cheap positive liveness for the target, and with the baseline check reduced the target scan is now roughly nine tenths of the backend on both machines, so it is no longer one candidate among several.
- [Fixed] Stop reading twenty process environments a second to answer a question the first one answers. The supervision loop asked whether anything recorded at launch still proves the game is alive, and it did so by reading every baseline PID and then testing whether none of them came back alive. A game under Proton leaves about twenty of those, so the check spent about twenty procfs environment reads a second while the first live PID already settled it. Measured on 2026-09-07 that was 2.0 points of one core on a Steam Machine and 5.6 on a Steam Deck, which was 61% of everything the backend spent while a session was live, and the two machines implied the same number of PIDs from per-call costs differing by three times. The check now stops at the first PID that is alive or unreadable, which is the same predicate rather than an approximation of it: one live PID settles the question and reading the rest cannot change the result. Proving the game gone stays exhaustive, because only the whole set can prove it, and an unreadable PID still counts as alive, because procfs denies a same-user non-dumpable process and reading that as gone would call a live game dead. A regression checks the new predicate against the old one over every arrangement of a mixed set.
- [New] Count the one-second baseline check per PID, which is what named it. A counter on the tick would have reported once a second and explained nothing: the loop runs once a second and calls the check once for every process recorded at launch, so what it costs is a question about how many of those there are. The count is now its own path beside the target scan, the panel's two calls and the process-table walk.
- [Fixed] Stop claiming the compositor writes a frame rate once a second. Read straight off the pipe, Gamescope wrote a rate-and-focus pair every 5.3 seconds on a Steam Deck and about every 7.5 on a Steam Machine, both steady, so a 90-second window holds a dozen readings and not ninety. The field notes and the probe's own comment both said once a second, which made every within-window spread look like it rested on ninety points, and one constant took it seriously enough to give a live pipe five seconds to speak before treating it as dead, which is less than a single write interval. The probe measures the interval and reports it beside the rate, warns when a spread rests on too few readings instead of presenting it as solid, and waits long enough to contain more than one write at the slowest cadence seen. Seen the same day from the other side as well: with no power limit the pipe read a steady 56 while the game's own overlay read a steady 38, so composited frames and application frames differ in both directions and neither substitutes for the other.
- [New] Repeat one workload on both machines and finally state a ratio that means something. Same title, same 142-record table, same GE-Proton11-2, same Cheat Engine by SHA, CE Decky 0.9.17 on both, panel hidden, three 90-second windows a side, the handheld at no power limit and brought to a comparable frame rate through its own quality settings. Against the Steam Machine the handheld pays 2.13 times for Cheat Engine, 1.88 on the wineserver group, 2.76 for CE Decky's own backend and 2.31 for the session as a whole, which is 22.3 points of one core against 9.7. The earlier pair of readings had suggested the backend differed by nearly four times, and the gap between that and 2.76 is what the uncontrolled game, table, Proton and power limit were worth. One figure does not move with the hardware at all: the target scan is 38.6% of the backend on one machine and 38.9% on the other, so the same code costs 2.7 times more per pass and spends it in the same proportions. The handheld's panel called the backend not once either, and 61% of its backend is unattributed in the same way. The row asking for this is deleted from Still worth checking rather than annotated.
- [New] See, rather than infer, what a desktop session's processor cost is paid out of. The package telemetry taken across the same three windows reports the GPU busy 93.5%, 94.0% and 93.5% at an unchanged 2.37 GHz, so the scene is GPU bound, while package power rose from 95.9 W unattached to 98.8 W attached and returned to 95.7 W. The frames not moving was already recorded; the mechanism was not, and a reading that shows load and clocks unchanged while watts rise is the difference between an observation and an explanation supplied by reasoning. It also says why the handheld reading is the one that decides whether any of this is worth recovering: a device held at a power limit has no spare headroom to pay from.
- [New] Settle where the backend's cost goes while a session is live, on the Steam Machine. Three 90-second windows with the panel hidden, two unattached either side of one attached, on the same title and 142-record table as the machine's recorded row: the panel's own detector called the backend not once in any of them, so with the panel hidden it is not a cost at all and the fear that it would mask an interval experiment is answered. The supervisor's target scan ran at exactly its three-second cadence and cost 1.29% of a core against the backend's 3.33%, and all of that is the process-table walk, whose own counter reports 1.286 against the scan's 1.287. Timed in the same window with a game actually running, that walk is 39.2 ms rather than the 13.9 ms measured with nothing to match, so the earlier figure was correctly labelled a floor, and multiplying by the cadence predicts 1.31% against the 1.287% the counters measured: two instruments agreeing rather than one checked against itself. What is now the largest open item is new, that 61% of the backend is still unattributed and the one-second baseline check at 0.86 ms per call cannot account for it, so the suspect is the supervision tick machinery rather than the work the tick does.
- [Fixed] Take the write out of the status read. A Cheat Engine registered before this plugin read versions was given its label inside `get_status()`, which the panel polls and which several helpers that promise to write nothing call for identity, so a measurement run could commit durable configuration for that one case. Only a configuration from an older build can be missing the label and nothing at runtime can produce another, so the whole question is settled once at load, which is an explicit mutation boundary, and a failure there is a missing label rather than a reason to fail load. The status call reports what is recorded and writes nothing, and an unusable registration is still left alone.
- [Fixed] Keep the measurement window one window when Decky is slow to answer. The opening counter read happened after the window was anchored and the frame and sensor readers had started, so a backend that took five seconds to reply shortened the process-CPU sample by five seconds while everything else kept the full interval, and the report went on presenting readings of different seconds as one window. That is worst exactly when the backend is struggling, which is when the correlation is worth having. The read now happens before the anchor, so an unresponsive Decky costs the run its own time and never a second of the sampling interval, and the time it took is reported beside the window rather than folded into it.
- [Fixed] Refuse to subtract counters that two different backends produced. The cost probe called a restart only when the reported uptime went backwards, which misses the ordinary case: a snapshot taken shortly after load, a restart, and a second snapshot well into the new process read exactly like one long window, and subtracting them mixes two incarnations into numbers that can look entirely plausible. Every counter snapshot now carries a token identifying the run it belongs to, generated once when the backend loads, and a delta is computed only when both snapshots agree on it. A snapshot whose schema this probe does not read, and an installed build too old to carry the token at all, are reported rather than read as if the fields still meant the same thing.
- [Fixed] Stop the preflight calling an ordinary Linux desktop the target. Its `target_ok` was Linux plus a process table, which every developer desktop has, so a machine that cannot observe Steam, Proton or Cheat Engine on SteamOS reported that it could, against this project's own documentation and against the workflow where development happens on a desktop and the device is somewhere else. The two questions are now separate: `target_host_ok` says a helper can run here at all, `target_ok` says this machine is the device and requires SteamOS, and a desktop answers yes to the first and no to the second with the remote-target arrangement named in the refusal. `procfs` also stopped meaning that a directory exists, which a container can present empty while every process reads as absent, and now means that the table can be read.
- [Changed] Say that accepted audit findings are one batch, not one errand each. The first-push audit told an agent to make each accepted finding an ordinary change with its own commit and QA, and "its own QA" reads as a gate run per finding: this run made two of them for two changes, one of which was documentation only. A commit per finding is still right, because that is what keeps the reason readable, but the validation is one selection over the finished batch followed by one push, which is the same cadence the rest of this contract already asks for and is exactly the sort of spent call the audit exists to find.
- [Changed] Take the registered Cheat Engine from the backend that registered it. The primitive bench asked the operator for the executable to hash, and the live backend already reports it, so producing a reading meant first reading the plugin's own configuration file from a developer helper: a second copy of a fact that has an authority. The path now comes from the same authenticated local API the install authority uses, is validated here rather than trusted, and is only asked for when the caller wants a different one. A backend that is not running costs that one row and says why, instead of ending a run that is still measurable.
- [Changed] Name the changelog helper where the rule that needs it is stated. `AGENTS.md` is read on every clean run and says that one version's entries are a single unbroken list, while `scripts/changelog.py`, which exists to make that true at the moment an entry is written, is documented in `docs/DEVELOPMENT.md`, a file the routing table opens for build and release work rather than for writing an entry. An agent therefore met the rule and never met the tool, hand-edited the file against an anchor that moves every round, and spent two red QA runs on the exact failure the helper prevents. The rule now names the command.
- [New] Hold the platform rule in a regression rather than in a habit. A helper that reads something only the target has now has to refuse a host that is not one, and the check finds it by looking for what it actually touches: procfs and the calls that do not exist on Windows at all. The same regression keeps a helper with no platform requirement from acquiring a guard it does not need, since repository QA, packaging, the changelog tool, the CT inspector probe and the TLS probe run everywhere and should keep doing so. It refuses a POSIX-only call at module scope, which is where the session cost probe used to fail before any refusal could be reached, and it requires the guard to sit after argument parsing, so `--help` still answers on a machine that cannot run the helper itself.
