The three helpers that answer for the host some other way are listed with the reason each one does, so a new name there has to be a reason and not a waiver.
- [New] Keep the per-call timings behind a tracked harness, so a figure can be repeated rather than only quoted. The numbers the backend-cost plan already carried came from a script written for one command on the device and not kept, which is exactly the failure the tracked-helper rule exists to stop. `scripts/target_primitive_bench.py` times the production primitives a live session repeats, the process-table walk, the supervisor's scan, the one-second baseline check, the Proton inventory and the Cheat Engine hash, reporting median and p95 of per-thread processor and wall time with the size of the process table beside them and what one pass costs at a one-second and a three-second cadence. It reproduced the recorded figures to within a few percent on the same machine, so those stand and can now be repeated on second hardware, and the plan's remaining step is a reading with a session live rather than an instrument that still has to be built. A primitive whose inputs a run was not given is left out rather than timed against an invented one.
- [New] Answer which machine a helper is running on, and refuse the wrong one in words. The same checkout is worked on from a Windows or Linux desktop, from CI and from the device, and nothing in the tree said which, so a target helper started on a desktop ran until it reached a POSIX-only call and left an agent reading a traceback about `os.sysconf` when the answer was that the work belongs elsewhere. `scripts/host_platform.py` names the operating-system family, whether this is SteamOS, and the Valve model, which has to come from DMI because SteamOS reports `VARIANT_ID=steamdeck` on a Steam Machine as well as on a Steam Deck: `Fremont` is the Steam Machine, `Jupiter` the Steam Deck LCD and `Galileo` the Steam Deck OLED. Every helper that needs the device asks first and refuses by name with its own exit code before doing any work, while `--help` keeps working everywhere, and helpers with no platform requirement are left alone. The preflight reports the machine and answers `repository_ok` and `target_ok` separately, because on a desktop those have different answers, and the session cost probe no longer reads the kernel tick at import, where nothing could have reported an unsupported host anyway.
- [New] Capture, in one window, everything the next device session cannot be asked for twice. The scarce resource is a person holding one scene still for several minutes, and a field not taken then cannot be taken afterwards. The session cost probe now also reads the backend's repeating-path counters at each end of its window and never inside it, per-process wakeups, runqueue delay, resident memory and byte counters where the kernel allows them, package power, GPU load, clocks and temperatures sampled across the window rather than at its ends because those are gauges, the CPU pressure delta, and the size of the process table, which the table walk's cost scales with and without which two machines are not comparable on the backend figure. Sensor channels are found by reading hwmon labels, so a device that exposes a set another one does not is read rather than assumed, and `/proc/<pid>/io` being denied for this plugin's own backend is recorded as the refusal it is rather than dropped, because an absent counter and a counter that did not move look identical otherwise.
- [New] Count what the backend's repeating paths actually run, so the cost of a live session can be attributed to one of them instead of argued about. CPU for the backend process is a single figure covering the one-second supervision loop, the three-second target scan, the worker threads they hand work to and whatever the panel asks for, and two of the cadences under suspicion are the same three seconds, so neither that figure nor a wakeup count for the task can name a path. Each repeating path now carries a monotonic count with the wall and per-thread processor time it spent, for the two calls the panel's detector makes, for the supervisor's own scan, whose known cadence is what proves a reading was sound at all, and for the process-table walk both of them run. Nothing is logged per call, because a line per invocation would add work at the rate being measured, and the snapshot increments nothing, so reading the counters never becomes one of the counts. The counters reach the support bundle as a field and as a plain-text block, which is where a report about a hot device or a drained battery has to be answerable from.

## 0.9.16 — 2026-09-07

- [Fixed] Wait for the second search to be pressable in the catalog roster regression. The test pressed Search again as soon as the first search's results were on screen, and the panel clears its busy flag one continuation later, in the enclosing runner's `finally`, so the press could land on a control that was still disabled: nothing started, the row went on showing the first search's completed count, and the assertion failed for a reason that was not the panel. It turned one exact-head CI run red while every local run passed. The regression now waits for the control to be enabled before pressing it, which is the state it is asserting about in the first place, and the panel itself is unchanged.
- [Changed] Settle the frontend detector by counting its calls, not by reading the backend's wakeups. The plan's first step called voluntary context switches decisive, and they are only decisive against the state where the backend does nothing at all: while a session is live the one-second supervision loop, the three-second target scan and the worker threads they launch are wakeups too, and a counter for the whole task cannot tell one three-second cadence from another. The step is now a small tracked instrumentation change carrying a monotonic counter for `get_ce_launch_capability`, for `get_runtime_status` and for the supervisor's target-scan passes, read by a tracked helper either side of the hidden-panel window, with no per-call logging and no snapshot taken through a counted path, so the frontend counters advancing or standing still is itself the answer and the supervisor's count proves the reading was sound. Wakeups, runqueue delay, pressure, resident memory and the power telemetry stay as corroboration and bounds rather than as path identity.
- [Changed] Hold the AppID a process declares in the cheap liveness check the plan proposes. Keeping a target's PID, start time and executable proves only that something of that name is alive at that PID, and two titles running at once can share an executable name and a Proton chain, so the candidate now also holds the `SteamAppId` or `SteamGameId` that the baseline check already matches on and requires all four to agree before the full table walk is skipped. A mismatch, a reused PID or an unreadable field falls back to the complete scan, and the cheap check may only ever confirm presence: the complete scan stays the only thing that may prove a target absent.
- [Changed] Correct what the backend-cost plan says runs during a session, before anything acts on it. The document listed the bridge heartbeat as steady-state work, though that poll ends when the bridge leaves its starting phase, and it left out the frontend's own three-second detector, which is not gated on panel visibility and calls a capability refresh that observes the game container twice, enumerates and hashes the Proton tools, and hashes the Cheat Engine executable on every call. Timed on the Steam Machine, that path implies more processor time than the supervisor scan the plan was going to A/B, so changing that interval first could have been masked by it entirely. The order is attribution first, the primitives are named, and what procfs refuses about the backend is recorded: its own syscall counters are denied, so the instrumentation list lost the item it was built around and gained wakeup counts, runqueue delay and pressure instead.
- [Fixed] Ask the provider surveys for the home only in the modes that read it. Making both helpers refuse a guessed Steam home resolved that home eagerly, so summarising a corpus already on this disk was refused for not naming where the plugin's index lives, and listing the library with an exact userdata path was refused for not naming where a provider cache it never opens would be. Each path resolves for the mode selected, an ordinary search and an ordinary collection keep refusing rather than guessing, and the four combinations have regressions.
- [Fixed] Name the machine the desktop figures came from. Both the field notes and the temporary backend document called it a 12-thread desktop, which is not an identity and invites reading a generic tower into it: it is a Valve Steam Machine, and the README already said so while the durable rows did not. The row now carries its processor and thread count the way the handheld row carries the Deck's, so a number and the machine that produced it stay together.
- [Fixed] Take the Steam home from the authority in the provider surveys, not from whoever is logged in. Removing a hard-coded stock home from the corpus and search surveys replaced it with the running account's home, which is the same mistake with a different spelling: `DECKY_USER_HOME` owns the Steam library, the provider cache and the maintained index, and a developer helper running as another account would have surveyed a different library and printed the result as evidence, where an empty answer from the wrong home reads exactly like an empty answer from the right one. Both helpers take `--user-home`, or the exact paths themselves, read `DECKY_USER_HOME` from the environment when it is set, and refuse with the command that reports it rather than falling back to a guess. The screenshot helper keeps the running account's home, which is what a developer output directory means.
- [Changed] Write down what the cost harness should capture before the next device session. Two measurements are owed and both will hold a scene still for several minutes while recording only CPU and a frame rate, and a field not taken then cannot be taken afterwards. `docs/BACKEND_POLL_COST.md` now lists what was confirmed readable as the ordinary user and why each one earns its place: per-process syscall counts, which are what polling is made of and may attribute the process-table walk arithmetically without installing a modified build; voluntary context switches as a second independent reading of the same rate; resident memory, which nobody would hold a scene to learn on its own; and package power, GPU load and clocks, which would let the claim that a handheld pays in frames rather than in spare capacity be observed instead of inferred. It also records what is not available, so no session is planned around frame pacing or around a pipe vocabulary nobody has enumerated.
- [Fixed] Stop the helpers teaching a home that only one device has. The launch probe's three runnable examples still passed the stock Steam home after its option help had been corrected, and three other helpers carried it as a default path for screenshots, the provider cache and the survey index. Examples name the exact home and where to read it. The screenshot helper's output directory follows the account the helper runs as, which is what that path means.
- [Fixed] Describe the measurements in the units the instrument actually reports. The probe was corrected to say that its `wineserver` group is every process of that name on the host and its game group an AppID cohort, but the field notes went on calling them the game's `wineserver` and the game's own process, which claims an identity nothing established. Each row now names the group and, where the reports show it, that the group held exactly one process in every window, which is the stronger statement and is checkable. The temporary backend document lost its cross-machine ratio column, its reading of the backend cost as polling rather than as cost that appears with a live session, and an estimate of frames recovered that no measurement supports.
- [Changed] Say that the two machine measurements are two readings, not one comparison. They differ in game, table size, Proton and power limit at once, and the durable wording called them the same session and named slower cores and the plugin's polling as the reason the backend differed. The probe measures whole processes and attributes nothing to a mechanism, so the rows now state what each device did under its own exact configuration, and the controlled repeat that would license a ratio is recorded as owed rather than implied. Both rows carry that warning rather than only the newer one, because a reader who lands on the desktop row first would otherwise learn nothing of it, and each says that the other ran a different title and a differently sized table without naming either.
- [Fixed] Take the target login and home from the device instead of assuming them. The remote procedure hard-coded the default Steam user and its home in every command, including the plugin root a first install names, which is the assumption the home-authority invariant exists to refuse and is wrong on any device set up differently. Commands carry the login, the mirror and the home as values of the run, an existing install takes user_home and settings_dir from the install authority, and a device with no install states the home it found rather than deriving one. Four campaign gate identifiers left in helper text went with them.
- [Fixed] Stop the remote mirror from being a checkout the workflow says it is not. The procedure excluded the Git directory from an rsync and said that exclusion was the guarantee, but an excluded path is protected from deletion as well as from transfer, so a Git directory already in that location outlives every sync and leaves a real checkout wearing a mirror's name. The mirror is cleared of it before the sync and checked after, and the check is what carries the guarantee.
- [Fixed] Keep a real stall in the frame readings. Partial readings were dropped by being smaller than one frame a second, while the comment said they were dropped for spanning a focus change, so a window that genuinely sat below one frame lost exactly the observation it existed to make. The rate is written before the focus it belongs to, so a reading is now paired with its focus and dropped by where it sits: the first of a window, and the one whose focus differs from the reading before it. The count of what was dropped is reported beside the rest.
- [Fixed] Stop publishing a session total that no session pays. The cost probe added Cheat Engine, the backend and `wineserver` into one figure per reading, and `wineserver` is running either way, so the number was larger than attaching costs and disagreed with every figure derived from these reports by subtracting one run from another. A reading now carries group CPU only, and the docstring says why there is no total, that the group holds every `wineserver` on the host rather than the game's alone with its process count as the tell, that the game group is a cohort including Steam's reaper, and that every run being compared takes the same identity flags. The examples pass the AppID on both.
- [New] Write down the one open question about what a session costs, and how to answer it. The backend's 12.1% of a core on a handheld is the largest single part of an attached session and the only part this project controls completely, and what it polls for, what each cadence buys and which lever is cheap are spread across three loops in the launch supervisor. `docs/BACKEND_POLL_COST.md` holds the figures from both machines, the three cadences and what each one is paid for in, the A/B that separates the process-table walk from the per-second work, and the reasons the obvious change would be wrong. It is temporary and the cleanup section names it: what it establishes goes to the field notes and it is deleted with the change that settles it.
- [Changed] Measure the desktop session with the same instrument, and correct a total that was short. Reproducing the original figures with the tracked harness returned Cheat Engine at 4.7 against the recorded 4.6 and a wineserver delta of 1.6 against 1.5, and the frame rate did not move across three runs that agreed to the second decimal, so both the old numbers and the new instrument stand. What the old accounting had no line for was this plugin's own backend, which costs 3.3% of a core here while a session is live, so the 6% it called a total was half as much again. With that part measured on both machines the comparison is finally on one basis: the same session costs about 9.6% of a core on the desktop and about 20% on the handheld, and the backend is the part that grows most between them, by nearly four times against a quarter for Cheat Engine itself.
- [Fixed] Keep the installed game out of the tree. The handheld measurement named the exact title and its AppID in the README, the field notes, the probe's own examples and its tests, which is one of the identities the publication rules say nothing public may carry, and the existing measurement rows deliberately describe a game rather than name one. The rows say what kind of title it was and what it was doing, which is what makes the number reproducible, and the examples and fixtures use placeholders.
- [Fixed] Compare the two machines on one accounting. The handheld measurement was published against the desktop figure as about 20% of a core versus about 6%, and those totals count different things: the desktop accounting has no line for CE Decky's own backend, which is the largest single part of the handheld number. On the parts both measurements contain, the handheld costs 8.2 against 6.1, about a third more rather than three times. What the backend costs on a desktop has never been measured, so the whole-session totals stay uncomparable and both documents now say so.
- [Fixed] Print launch-probe examples that can be run. All three examples in the Cheat Engine launch probe omitted the Proton tool ID, which the probe requires for every launch and refuses to guess, so following the documentation produced a refusal. The examples carry it, the option says it is required for a launch, and the refusal points at the identities it already lists in that same report rather than at a second command.
- [Changed] Report the two paths the other probes ask for, from the authority that can actually answer. The state and launch probes want DECKY_USER_HOME and the plugin's settings directory, and the help text sent the reader to the environment probe, which reads procfs bytes SteamOS may deny to a same-user sibling. The live backend reports both beside the plugin root in the answer the install authority already reads, so it now carries them, withholding either one that is not shaped like Decky's own directory rather than passing it on. The offline record reports them as absent, because deriving them from the root would be a guess.
- [Changed] Say how to install onto a device that has never held this plugin. The install rules describe resolving a root from Decky's live inventory, from a legacy sibling layout and from the durable record, and say nothing about the case where all three are empty because nothing was ever installed. The first install on such a device names its plugin root explicitly and omits the replace flag, and every install after it resolves the root on its own.
- [Changed] Require a measurement's harness to be a tracked helper before the number is recorded. The preservation rule protects research code from being deleted without a home, and it cannot reach a tool that was never committed: the harness behind the desktop cost figures was written in a scratch directory on the device and disappeared with it, leaving a number nobody could reproduce or repeat on second hardware. Recording a measurement now waits on its tool living under `scripts/` with its command documented.
- [Changed] Say what a session costs on a handheld, having measured it. The figures were from a 12-thread Steam Machine and the field notes carried the handheld case as an open question with an expectation attached to it. A Steam Deck LCD held at a 5 W limit, with a single-player title steady at 39.4 fps and a 3-record table with no cheat enabled, pays about 20% of one core against about 6% for the same work on the desktop, and it comes out of frames: 39.4 falls to 37.4, which is 2 frames, while the two unattached windows either side differ by 0.1 of a frame. The largest single part is not Cheat Engine at 5.8% but CE Decky's own backend at 12.1% while a session is live. The README states both machines, and the open question is removed rather than annotated.
- [New] Say how to work a SteamOS device across the network. The SteamOS section is written for the machine the checkout is on, and a Steam Deck reached from another machine, which is where development ordinarily happens, is neither of the two answers it offers. `docs/REMOTE_TARGET.md` carries that case: the helpers import `py_modules`, so the working tree is mirrored to the device rather than a script copied to it; the copy is a copy and nothing is committed from it; every remote command carries its own working directory; and a Decky Loader older than the running Steam client stays an active service whose frontend never reaches the quick access menu, which is a version to read before anything else rather than a fault to debug.
- [New] Measure what a session costs on the machine it is running on. Every figure in the field notes came from one 12-thread Steam Machine, taken by hand in a campaign whose documents have since been deleted, so there was no route to the same numbers on a handheld and no record of how the first ones were produced. `scripts/target_session_cost_probe.py` samples every process twice across one bounded window, groups the CPU time into the parts a session is made of, and reads the frame rate from the live Gamescope statistics pipe over that same window, so processor cost and frame cost come from one window rather than two runs assumed comparable. It counts nothing for a process that started or exited inside the window, reports both counts so a run that raced a launch is visible, and reports zero frame samples rather than a number when the compositor was not drawing.

## 0.9.15 — 2026-09-06

- [Changed] Make an agent settle where it is running before acting on it. The SteamOS section is headed by a condition and said nothing about establishing it, so an agent either assumes the device is not there and leaves every claim about Steam, Proton and Cheat Engine unverified when one was, or assumes it is and produces target claims from a host that cannot make them. The section now says to answer it out of the tracked helpers rather than from a path or a user name, names which helper answers which half, and asks for the answer in the first report of the run. The preflight also moved to the start of a run that may reach the device rather than to the first mutation, which is where the question was being discovered.
- [Changed] Prove a regression against an earlier revision without writing that revision into the working tree. A review round ends by running the new tests with the production source rolled back to the reviewed head, because a test that passes on the commit it was written against says nothing about the defect it was written for, and there was no tool for it: it was done by hand, seven times in one run, by copying baseline source over tracked files and copying them back afterwards, in a workflow whose every round ends in a commit, a push and an install onto a device. `scripts/qa_baseline_check.py` does it in a temporary worktree with this checkout's tests on top, names the tests that failed there, and refuses to read either a selection that passes or a run that never started as a proved regression.
- [Fixed] Clear both halves of a failed download in the press that offers to. A rejected transfer writes twice, once as this session's own memory that the row is damaged and once as the durable record the backend keeps, and the retry press took the durable one and stopped there: the row came straight back as Damaged with a second Retry beside it and nothing on screen to say why one press had not been enough. A row is also no longer counted for history it is not showing, so a saved copy this row produced, offered as Local with both presses live, stops offering to clear the older revision behind it. The badge, the two presses and the count are now one reading of the same records rather than four.
- [Fixed] Offer Retry over the marks the screen is showing, not the ones it has stopped showing. A row whose source has replaced its file and advertised a checksum for it has outlived its old records: nothing is refused, the row says nothing and both presses are live. It still counted toward the retry press, so the panel offered to clear one mark under a list where no row carried one, and the press cleared a record the reader had never been shown. A row counts now only while it is presenting a mark, decided by what decides its badge and its presses, and a row that is presenting one still clears its whole history in a single press.
- [Fixed] Say that a checksum has changed, not that there is none. A saved copy found by the row it came from was described the same way whether the source publishes no checksum at all or publishes one that now names different bytes, and on the second the sentence was simply false. Those are different answers, and the one being withheld was the stronger: not that nobody can tell whether the online version has changed, but that it has. The window says which of them it is, and offers the same two presses either way.
- [Fixed] Let a source that says what it will serve answer for its own past. A download that turned out not to be a table, and an archive nothing here could open, are recorded against the exact digest of what arrived, and search then refused the row on either of them however long ago it happened. A provider that replaces the file under the same row and advertises a checksum for it has said directly that these are not those bytes, so the record is about a revision this row no longer offers and the refusal now moves with the digest: the advertised one being on the record still refuses the download, and one naming anything else refuses nothing. Only a file the source says it no longer has, which never produced bytes to record, stays a fact about the row. A source that advertises no checksum is unchanged, because nothing there can tell an old failure from the current file.
- [Fixed] Keep every record a provider row has earned, and let the newest one speak. A row serves several revisions of a table over the years and each can be recorded as not working in its own right, but the search screen kept one mark per row: whichever the loop wrote last stood for all of them. A source saying it no longer has the file could therefore sit behind an older revision that came straight back off, so the row offered a download the record should have stopped, showed the wrong reason, and took one press of Retry per record with the next appearing only after the first was cleared. Every mark is now kept, newest first, and what each one refuses is read from its own cause: bytes that came straight back off refuse those bytes, a source that has lost the file or served something that was not a table refuses the download, and Retry clears the whole of a row's history in one press.
- [Fixed] Keep asking the source again available for a table refused on this device. One not-working record is keyed by its exact SHA and again by every provider row those bytes came from, so that the mark shows on a source advertising no digest, and reading both as one answer refused the download along with the copy: a table that came straight back off for an older build of the game is exactly the one whose source may have published a fix, and the press that would find that out was the one being disabled. A provider-row mark naming the saved copy's own digest now refuses nothing about the download, a mark naming anything else still does, and a saved table refused on its own opens instead of being retired, with the ring on the download and the copy's own refusal said beside it.
- [Fixed] Stop a source's failure taking the copy already on this device with it. A file the provider no longer has, a download whose bytes were not a table, or a transfer this session found damaged retired the whole row, and the local table was folded into that row rather than listed beside it, so a table the user had downloaded months ago became unreachable the moment its source broke. That is the moment it is worth the most. Each half of the press is now gated by the record that is actually about it: what the row serves disables Download again and says why, a mark against the saved copy's own exact SHA disables Use saved copy and leaves the download, and the row is retired only when it has neither half left.
- [Fixed] Name the three ways a saved table and the row offering it can be related, instead of two. The window that stands between a saved copy and the network was told only whether a digest had matched, so everything that was not a digest match was described as a provider checksum that matched, on sources that publish no checksum at all. It now says which case it is: a checksum that matches these bytes, a copy downloaded from this source before on a source that publishes none so nothing can tell whether the online version has changed, or a saved table opened on its own. Use saved copy and Download again stay the two presses either way.
- [Fixed] Keep a refused repository search out of the deadline that refuses the whole of GitHub. Repository search is metered apart from the release and tree reads that actually produce the rows, so recording that refusal as the source's own cooldown had the next search of any game turned away before it could read the index already on this device, for a budget one of three routes spends. It is held against that one route now, and the source's own cooldown stays what it was: what a refused release or tree read writes.
- [Fixed] Spend one GitHub search request on a refusal, not one per alias. A search asks for an index per game alias, and the refusal was met again on each: the second alias raised it with nothing cached behind that one, and the whole source failed carrying the candidates the first alias had just recovered from its own stale copy. The refusal is now latched for as long as GitHub asked, every alias after it is served from whatever copy is on this disk, and an alias with no copy costs that alias and nothing else.
- [Fixed] Say what a row with no checksum behind it can and cannot prove, and let the user decide. Two of the four sources publish no digest, so a table already downloaded from one of them could only be recognised by the row it came from, and that row was then opened silently as though the bytes had been identified: an artifact ID is not content identity, and the same row can serve a changed file. Deciding it the other way, by always downloading, is worse for the person it happens to, who has the table and no network. The press now offers the copy and says plainly that this source publishes no checksum, so nothing here can tell whether the row still serves these bytes, with Download again beside it. Which of several revisions the row offers is the most recently retrieved one rather than whichever was listed first, and the others keep their own rows.
- [Fixed] Answer a GitHub search from the index this device already has when the search budget runs out. Repository search is metered apart from the rest of the API, so an exhausted budget is a refresh that cannot be made rather than a source that has stopped answering, which is what the seven-day stale window is for: it was discarded anyway, taking with it the release and tree reads that were still being served. The stale index is now used, the deadline GitHub asked for is written down first so the search does not clear it, and a refusal with no stale copy behind it still fails the source.
- [Fixed] Let the reader open the line that says why a session is not usable. The runtime row stops being a focus stop where it says something short and complete, and that was decided from whether it carried the live-state hint: a table Cheat Engine could not open, whose text ends in a message the backend wrote, and a recovered Cheat Engine running a bridge this build replaced both have no hint, so both became a truncated row with no controller press anywhere able to finish it. The line and whether it can be read where it stands now come out of the same branch, so only the two states that say a complete short fact are read in place.
- [Fixed] Keep the ring on the Configure cheats pager at both ends of the list. Pressing Next onto the last page disables Next under the thumb that was on it, and Previous onto the first page does the same at the other end, which drops the ring and leaves the window with nothing focused. It is the defect the code window already carries a fix for, in the footer this version gave to Configure cheats. Which control turned the page is recorded at the press and the ring is put back after the page commits, on the control beside it when that one has just disabled itself, and never when the section, the filter or the scripts switch moved the page without a press.
- [Fixed] Refuse a field the public-release cleanup removed coming back in a fixture. The managed-CE and CE-launch payloads lost their campaign gate field, and the test fixtures went on emitting it: the ordinary type-check reads `src` and not `tests`, so a fixture declared as the production interface while carrying a property that interface no longer has passed every route. One fixture was exactly that. The fixtures are corrected, and repository QA now refuses the key in any tracked tree that builds an object, production and test alike, while leaving the assertion that the backend stopped sending it and the comment explaining why.
- [Changed] Say which frontend the device is actually running. The install authority reported the plugin root and the version, and a development version is rebuilt and reinstalled many times without moving, so answering whether the device held the build in front of you meant reaching into Decky's own directory for a digest by hand. It now reports the installed bundle's SHA-256 beside the root, and a bundle it cannot read reports nothing rather than refusing the root. Written down with it: where Steam's own UI source is on the device and which file in it decides where a controller press sends the focus ring, which this run had to work out from scratch and which nothing in this repository or in the Decky typings records.
- [Changed] Stop resting the controller ring on Home's lines of state. A row with nothing to press is otherwise a focus stop of its own, which is what lets a screen made almost entirely of such rows be scrolled through and lets a row whose text is clipped be opened, and on a panel of controls it meant paging past stops that showed nothing for being reached. The runtime line while nothing is connected, the pinned-controls note and the reason a table cannot start yet are now read where they are. The runtime line keeps its stop while it carries the reason a connected session could not be read, which is the one text there worth opening.
- [Fixed] Return the controller ring to the row that opened an Advanced screen. Table sources, Tables that did not work, Plugin data, Look inside and Debug replace the panel rather than sitting over it, so leaving one rebuilt every row behind it and the ring reopened at the top of a screen the reader had scrolled well past to reach that press. Which press opened the screen is now what says where the ring goes back.
- [Changed] Enter the table-search and Configure cheats paging footers through Next while keeping every footer action on its left/right path.
- [Fixed] Stop pulling the Home focus ring onto Advanced after a busy transition. The panel no longer sets an initial focus of its own, so opening it keeps Steam's normal one, and the action row states Advanced as its preferred entry instead.
- [Changed] Land on Advanced when the controller steps down into the Home action row. Steam enters a row at the control covering most of the width of the one above it, and reads a row's stated entry preference only when the movement carries no rectangle to match against, which is why that preference does place the ring on Next in this plugin's own modal footers and did nothing on this row. Advanced now leads the row and takes its free width, so it is that control. While a launch is waiting to be cancelled the cancel action is wider and the step lands there, which is the action that state is for.
- [Fixed] Focus the first readable section as soon as Look inside finishes loading the table index.
- [Changed] Put the Home Disable all and Advanced actions, and the compact game-picker actions, on one left/right controller path each.
- [Changed] Keep Previous, Next and Close in the table-search footer on one left/right controller path.
- [Changed] Keep all four Configure cheats footer buttons on one left/right controller path, and give the game picker a full-width selector whose menu stays within the modal.
- [Fixed] Keep plugin installation authority through repository cleaning and SteamOS procfs restrictions. The installer now resolves the exact root from Decky's authenticated inventory and the backend's own path, bootstraps an installed older build through Decky's validated sibling-directory contract, and retains its offline fallback in per-user XDG state instead of disposable build output.
- [Changed] Put Previous, Next, Apply and Cancel in one compact Configure cheats footer.
- [Changed] Cache bounded normalized GitHub repository-index answers for one day, with a seven-day stale fallback when refresh is unavailable, while keeping release and tree reads live.
- [Changed] Keep tables already associated with the current game in the completed search result list when providers fail or the device is offline, and default local rows to Use existing while offering an explicit Download again choice.
- [Fixed] Return controller focus to the exact table-code section row after closing its preview.
- [Fixed] Run the parsers when the answer they were written against changes. A captured provider answer under the test fixtures selected no test at all, so re-capturing a page after a site changed its markup passed a routed run in which nothing that reads it had run. A fixture now selects every test whose source names it, and that is held by a test of its own, so the routing cannot quietly stop covering them again. The focus assertions in the table-code window also stopped racing the commit that moves the ring, which is a third shape of the same test-side race already documented.
- [Changed] Delete the prototype tree. Forty-seven spike modules, their tests and their TypeScript models are all superseded by production code, by an integration 0.9.0 removed, or by a feature the design lists as post-v1. What they established that was still true and stated nowhere else is now in the durable contracts, and the provider answers they captured are regression inputs. The guard that refuses a production import of a prototype stays: the tree can only come back by somebody restoring one, and that is the defect worth refusing.
- [Changed] Test the two newest table sources against what they actually served. Their parsers were covered only by inputs written to suit them, while the captured answers sat unused in the prototype archive: the site's own sitemap index and page, a post carrying its real mirror markup, a repository search answer whose rows include ones that have to be dropped, and a tree listing whose files carry git object names rather than content digests. All five are now regression inputs, so a parser that keeps passing its hand-built cases and stops reading a real answer fails here rather than on a device.
- [Changed] Keep what the prototype archive established, and drop what stopped being true. Three projects the design names as reuse sources now carry the commit their behaviour was read from, an upstream failure mode explains why readiness is a heartbeat and never a visible window, and the reason in-process table replacement is not reached through Cheat Engine's trainer-mode load flag is recorded with the consent boundary it would cross. The archived matcher demonstration is deliberately not kept: its thresholds describe the prototype and not the shipped matcher, whose own numbers differ, and the rule it illustrated is already normative.
- [Fixed] Fail on a routing rule that has stopped naming anything. The validation router dropped a selected test that did not exist on its way out, so a rule whose test had been deleted still looked like a working rule: it selected nothing, and the change it existed to cover ran no test at all. That is what a rule deleted with the campaign runbook had been doing. The selection is no longer filtered, and every test a rule names by hand is held to a file that exists, so the next such rule fails the ordinary run instead of quietly covering nothing.
- [Changed] Remove three development scripts nothing can use any more. One validated a target-session handoff that no tool produces since the session tooling went; one prepared a Proton sidecar fixture for an integration 0.9.0 deleted; and the all-surfaces audit wrapper ran the release profile followed by the prototype suite, whose own commands are documented where the prototypes are. Their tests go with them, and the routed validation no longer selects a runbook test that no longer exists.
- [Fixed] Let the installer answer where it installed, without a second tool. Reading back the last verified install root meant reading this command's own standard output, which something else had to have captured into a session directory first; deleting that session tooling left the read half with nothing to find on any fresh checkout. Every successful install now writes its own bounded record, the read verifies that the recorded directory is still a real installation, and two retained records naming different roots that both still exist is reported as an ambiguity rather than resolved by guessing.
- [Changed] Refuse a release against the list of what is unverified, not against a registry of campaign gate IDs. The temporary tracker, runbook and gate contract are deleted, and what they established that is still true now lives in one publication document. The release check reads that document's own list of work still owed: while it holds a row, a stable tag is refused and names the rows; emptying it is what permits one. There is no second list to keep in step, and settling something means deleting its row rather than annotating it.
- [Changed] Run the test that covers the managed Cheat Engine capability when that capability changes. Its only exact assertion, including that the payload carries no URL or command key, sat in a file the ordinary validation route stopped selecting, so a leaked installer URL would have reached a developer's green run. The test now lives beside the module it asserts. Tests reach the release tooling from any working directory, and the session store's garbage collection has the regression it never had: ordinary play prepares a table snapshot per launch, and nothing proved the collector bounds them or that it leaves a directory it cannot identify alone.
- [Fixed] Keep the check that refuses a release the hardware has not answered for. The registry of open target questions moved out of the shipped plugin, and moving it took its own tripwire with it: nothing required the file to exist any more, and the release check reads a missing registry as nobody having any questions left. Deleting it would have silently permitted a stable tag with both questions still open. The file is required again, so removing it fails the build instead, and a registry that is present but yields no rows is now refused as a damaged file rather than read as an all-clear.
- [Changed] Cut the published screenshots with the tool that takes them. Each tile was a hand-placed crop of a whole captured screen, and the screens were kept in the tree so a tile could be recut without the device, which made the published set depend on four edited 4K frames and on somebody repeating a framing decision by eye. `target_screenshot.py --tile` now cuts one plugin window straight out of the live composited frame, losslessly and at the frame's own scale: the width is Steam's own layout geometry, so every modal tile is the same width and the panel is the panel, and only the height is measured, by finding the window's own edges rather than by assuming the game behind it is dark. Steam's button-hint bar is kept out of that, the equal margin each tile carries is added by the same command, and `--trim-bottom` is what stops the diagnostics screen above this device's prefix paths. The whole-screen copies are gone with the framing decisions that needed them.
- [Fixed] Run the tests that cover a changed frontend file. The ordinary validation route picked the component suite for a changed test file, the provider catalog, or one of five core modules, so a change to a modal got a type-check and a build and none of the eighteen files that render it. Which of `src/` a component test reaches is not a question a list in the runner can answer, and keeping one is what got it wrong: the suite imports two dozen modules from there, screens and helpers alike, so any change under `src/` runs it. A pass that skips the only stage covering the changed file says nothing about the change it was asked about.
- [Changed] Give the process choice the width of the row it is on. Steam gives an inline control the narrowest column its content will accept, and what this one holds is a Windows executable name with a note after it: `Grounded2-WinGDK-Shipping.exe · running` in a third of a row is a name cut through the middle, on the control that decides what Cheat Engine attaches to. Widening it in place would only move that onto the sentence beside it, which is the one that says what to do when nothing is running, so the control goes under its label and both get the whole row, like the button below it.
- [Fixed] Leave a blank line between the last entry of a version and the heading under it. The helper that writes these puts the first entry of a new section directly on the next version's heading, because a heading written a moment ago carries its blank line above that heading rather than under its own entries: the two releases then run together, which Markdown reads one way on some renderers and another way on the rest. It is the same defect as a blank line inside one version's list, from the other end, and it is now refused by the same tests.
- [Fixed] Put the focus ring where the next press is, in the window that shows what a table would run. Reading a script is one press repeated, and the ring opened at the top of the panel: every reader began by walking the stick the length of the window to find Next, and the last page then disabled Next under the thumb that was on it, which drops the ring entirely and leaves a window with nothing focused. The ring now opens on Next, follows whichever paging control the reader is using, and moves to the way out of the window once there is no page after this one. The row those controls sit on rebuilds itself the first time both of them can be pressed, which is what used to lose the ring mid-read, and putting it back is what survives that.
- [Fixed] Say what a row can act on, not how it came to be able to. Two of the four sources advertise no content digest at all, so every table already imported from either of them came back through search with nothing on the row to say so and was downloaded again to reach bytes the device already had. A row holding a table this game can use now says Local and offers it, whether a digest proved those bytes or the row is simply where they came from. Imported is what is left when the origin is known and nothing usable is attached to the row, which is the row that still has to go and fetch one. A digest that resolves to a local table is matched whatever case the provider wrote it in.
- [Changed] Give the search screen its bottom row back. Paging and the way out were three full-width buttons stacked under the results, on a panel that shows six of them: a third of the modal's height spent on two presses and a third that is the same press everywhere in the product. They are one row now, the pager centred with the page number between the two controls that change it, and Close against the right edge where this panel already puts a row's own controls. The page number leaves the status line above, which had been wrapping to two lines to hold it.

## 0.9.14 — 2026-09-05

- [Fixed] Always ask a provider profile, whatever the tree looks like. The reuse this version added skips a stage whose inputs have not moved, and the two profiles that exist to observe a live source were left in its scope: an unchanged tree says nothing about whether a provider has started refusing, changed its markup or moved a route, which is the whole question those profiles are asked. They now run every time, like packaging, and the decision itself is covered end to end rather than only the parts it is made from.
- [Changed] One full gate, and a stage that has already answered is not asked again. There were two profiles running the same stages, the second adding half a second of packaging, and their names invited running both: one read as the ordinary gate and the other as something kept for releases, while every production change here ends in an install that wants the package. A name for a choice nobody should make is worse than no name, so there is one gate now and it packages. Alongside it, the rule this project already states, that a pass is reused until a covered input changes, is applied by the runner instead of by whoever is typing: a stage whose fingerprint is unchanged since it passed is reported as reused and not run. The fingerprint is the contents of every tracked file, because the gate rewrites the bundle it built and a timestamp would have it invalidating its own reuse each time it ran, plus the stage's own command, its interpreter and the restored dependency trees' markers, so an edit anywhere invalidates everything. Packaging is never reused, an explicitly named test or stage always runs, and a second full gate on an unchanged tree costs 0.7 seconds against 84.
- [Fixed] Take when a source's own work began from that work, not from the fan-out around it. Every source runs at once and the diagnostics are written after all of them have finished, so reconstructing when one began from how long it took gives the moment the whole set ended minus that duration: those agree only when it was the last to finish. One slow source holding the others open therefore moved the reconstructed start past a refusal that really had landed while the job ran, and the success cleared it after all. Each job now carries the moment it started, so a refusal keeps the only thing it has to be judged by.
- [Fixed] Keep a refusal that carries no deadline, when the search that succeeded never saw it. A rate limit has an end written on it and survives on that; a challenge has only when it happened, so a search succeeding afterwards erased the one record that this device had been refused at all. Background maintenance now stamps when it was refused, and a search whose own answer began before that leaves the refusal, and the status that came with it, exactly as it found them. A refusal from before the search began is still cleared by it, because that is one the search has overtaken.
- [Fixed] Let a refusal outlive the search that succeeded anyway. One search makes several requests and a source can refuse one while answering the rest: an index refused with 429 while the game's own pages were served is exactly that, and the search then answered from the index this device already had. Recording that success cleared the deadline the refusal had just written, so the next search walked straight back into it. A deadline the source itself asked for now stands until it runs out, with the reason beside it, and keeping an index current no longer counts as one of the searches the screen reports, which had made a single press appear as two.
- [Fixed] End a background listing pass that has decided to stop, rather than starting its allowance again. The decision to give way was made inside the retry loop and only left that loop, so the loop around it began the whole five minutes afresh: a pass that woke from a cooldown into searches that never stop gave way, came back, and gave way again without end. The wait also only looked at its own limit after sleeping out the interval it waits in, so giving way overshot that limit by up to ten seconds every time.
- [Changed] Hold the shape of the source snapshot CI publishes. The authoritative run packs a tracked-source ZIP of the exact head it validated before it restores anything or runs a stage, so a run that fails later still carries it, and every part of how that is done is load-bearing while looking like something a tidy-up could simplify: the merge commit a pull request checks out is not the head anybody reviewed, `fetch-depth: 2` is what puts the real head in the object store beside it, `git archive` takes the commit rather than the working tree, and `if-no-files-found: error` turns a snapshot that was not built into a failed run instead of an empty artifact. Repository QA refuses each of those being lost, and refuses this workflow being given permission to delete artifacts, because a short retention and GitHub's own expiry are the whole of the cleanup.
- [Fixed] Switching a source off stops the request it already has out. The switch was checked before starting background work and again before writing anything down, and neither stops one in flight: a user who switches a source off is entitled to have this device stop talking to it, not to have it stop once the current answer arrives.
- [Fixed] Four defects in this version's own provider work. A background listing pass asleep on a cooldown woke straight into its request, so a search started while it slept was competing with it again; it asks whether a search is running immediately before every request now. A refresh of Playground's site index that came back malformed, or could not be written to disk, escaped as a failure of the whole source even though the copy already loaded was answering the search; neither costs the search its answer, and one that could not be written leaves the index due for another look rather than counted as checked. A server error counted as proof that the copy on disk was current, which hid a changed index for six hours behind a server that was briefly unwell; only a not-modified, or a refresh actually read, proves that. And a background refresh that was refused with a rate limit or a challenge said nothing to the provider's own state, so the next search walked into the source that had just said no.
- [Security] Show a script as the file composed it. Every line was put into NFC first, which is right for a control label, where two labels that look the same must not differ only in how they were composed, and wrong here: this is the screen that says what an exact SHA will execute, a decomposed sequence inside a Lua string is what Cheat Engine reads, and recomposing it changed the text while reporting nothing changed. Only the two changes that are counted and said on screen are made now, the cut at the byte ceiling and the removal of characters that would let a script render as something it is not.
- [Changed] Write down how to tell which source made a search slow. The backend log says how long a search took and what each source returned, and nothing in it says where the time went; the per-provider `last_latency_ms` in the diagnostics does, and it is what turned a 45 second search into one source at 44.8 against another at 2.4. That reading order is documented now, with the cache-file timestamps that say where inside a source the time went, and with the command that waits for a run on an exact head when the maintainer has asked for one.
- [Changed] Write a changelog entry with a command instead of by anchoring an edit. The mechanical half of every round is the same: find the newest version heading and put the line directly above the one under it, with no blank line between them, because a blank line renders the whole section as a loose list. Done by hand it is an anchored edit against text that changes every round, and the anchor is what fails. `python scripts/changelog.py add` does that, and refuses a label that is not one of the five, an em dash, more than one line, and a heading the version in `package.json` has moved past.
- [New] Say what a long search is doing, not only how long it has taken. A search is tens of seconds of somebody else's network on a device with no way to see any of it: one measured here took 45 seconds, of which Playground was 44.8 and 37 of those were its own 11.9 MB site index, while the other four sources had all answered inside three; the screen said "45s" and nothing else, which is indistinguishable from a panel that has stopped answering. Past ten seconds, which an ordinary search finishes well inside, the status line names each source that is still working and what it is doing, which is what says the wait belongs to one source rather than to the search, counts the ones that have answered and the ones that could not, and says which the user has switched off. It is one bounded read every two seconds while a search is running and none at any other time, and a read that fails leaves the elapsed time where it was: nothing about the search itself depends on being able to describe it.
- [Security] Stop a page of code depending on a guess about a font. The code view cuts a long line into rows at an estimated width, and the estimate listed the ranges known to be drawn at two columns: every family nobody listed, emoji and the symbol blocks among them, counted as one, so a page of them drew more rows than the count believed and the bottom of it went under the block's own `overflow` where no press could reach it and nothing said it was there. The default is the other way round now, one column up to the end of Cyrillic and Armenian and two above it, and the page is drawn as one element per row that cannot wrap at all: what the estimate decides is where a line is cut, not whether the page fits.
- [Fixed] Count the values a narrowed list is showing apart from the one it kept. The value a record is set to is always on offer, even when the search does not name it, and it takes the place of a match rather than an extra slot: the line under the list went on counting the two together, so it said it was showing all 24 matching values while it was showing 23 of them, and said nothing matched beside a value that was still on screen. The two are counted separately now, and the line says when the kept value is there because it is the record's own.
- [Security] Keep an embedded payload out of a window's body wherever the file writes it. A window is read out as text and its body is its whole subtree flattened, and the walk stopped at the window: excluding a payload written directly inside `<Forms>` left one written a single element deeper rendered as part of the window, and never listed as the file it is. Review counted an embedded file that the screen beside it then had nothing to show for, with the payload's own bytes on that screen inside something else. The payload container is not a window either, so `<Forms><Files>` is read as what it says it is. A payload is now left out of every window body and listed as its own unreadable section from wherever it was written, and the three readers of those bytes agree about all of it.
- [Fixed] Keep the pages a source had already read when the search runs out of time. A provider builds its answer as it goes and hands it over at the end, and the deadline cancels it: up to forty-five seconds of real pages went with the cancelled work, and the source was reported as having answered nothing. What it had found is now the answer, and the source is reported as having stopped part way through, which is the state a provider that breaks out of its own page loop on a rate limit already reports. The timeout also stopped writing a failure with no deadline on it, which is what cleared a cooldown the same source had just been given.
- [Fixed] Stop the background listing crawl taking the user's searches down with it. Per-page freshness comes due for most of the FearLess index at once, so a pass walked nearly the whole forum: measured here, one began with 247 of 332 pages pending and was rate limited three seconds in, and a rate limit belongs to the provider rather than to the crawl, so the searches the user was running got nothing from a source whose complete 332 page listing was sitting on the disk. A pass now refreshes at most 32 pages and no more than one starts every half hour, which still refreshes the whole listing inside the day its freshness allows; two refusals end a pass rather than one page being retried for as long as the provider keeps saying no, which is what re-armed that cooldown over and over.
- [Changed] Let a search have the provider first. The listing crawl asks for nothing while a search is running or has just finished, because both are asking one source for its one budget of requests and only one of them has somebody watching a screen. Giving way has an end: a pass held off for five minutes drops itself, and the pacing above decides when another starts.
- [Changed] Say that a source is being told to wait, rather than that it has nothing. A source in cooldown answered no rows and was reported as unavailable, which reads on the results line exactly like a source with no tables for this game. It now says it is rate limited, with the seconds until it can be asked again, and its refusal carries how many topics its cached listing matched, which is the one useful thing it still knows. The wait a download sits through is named as the source's own cooldown too, so the countdown beside Cancel says what it is counting down to.
- [Changed] Stop asking Playground about an index this device fetched hours ago. The conditional request was made on every search, which is a round trip for an answer already known, and while the site is answering slowly it is the whole of the short refresh window every time. A copy less than six hours old is now used without asking at all, and being told a copy is unchanged stops this process asking again for that long: proving it is current by fetching nothing would otherwise have to be recorded by rewriting eleven megabytes. A timestamp that is missing, unreadable or in the future counts as old, because a cache written by something that recorded none and a clock that has moved are both reasons to ask rather than to stop refreshing for good. What an out-of-date copy costs is narrow: its entries are cheat pages, so it is a table for a game that had no cheat page at all when the copy was written, while a game already in it is found exactly as before.
- [Changed] Answer Playground from the site index this device already has. That index is 11.9 MB across 50000 games, and reading it took 37 of one measured search's 45 seconds because the copy upstream had changed eleven minutes earlier, so the conditional request came back with the whole file rather than a not-modified: the search sat through a download that was about keeping the index current and not about the game being searched for. The copy on disk answers in 30 milliseconds and is what the search uses; the conditional request is still made, because a not-modified is usually immediate and a fresh index is better, but it gets five seconds of its own rather than the search's whole allowance, and one that cannot answer in that time is fetched behind the search and is ready for the next one. A device with no index at all still waits for its first, because there is nothing else to answer from.
- [Fixed] Do not start the second pass of a search on what is left of a spent budget. When the game's own installed directory names it better than the library entry does, a search runs its providers again against that name, sharing what remains of the one allowance. Started with a sliver of it, every source in that pass times out at once, so a user whose first pass had simply found nothing was shown a screen of failed sources instead. It now needs a remainder worth having, and does what it did before when it has one.
- [Fixed] Follow the game the panel is on rather than the search it was watching. The name of the watched search was held where a change to it could not rebuild the read that uses it, and switching game while a long search was still out leaves the screen saying it is searching straight through the change: the read went on asking about the search for the game the user had just left, which is answered to nobody else, so the new game's search ran with only a clock to show for it. The name is part of what the read is now, and the screen drops the search it was watching in the same commit that changes game, before the new one starts.
- [Fixed] Answer a search's progress to that search's own caller. The record has one owner and had no name, so with two searches at once, which nothing in the backend forbids and one panel serializing its own presses does not prevent, the caller of the first was shown the second's sources and the second finishing blanked the first's line while it was still running. The screen names the search it starts and is told about that one or about nothing.
- [Fixed] Stop a progress read from starting beside one that has not answered. It was a fixed two-second interval, so a read slower than that had another begun alongside it: two answers can arrive in the other order and put an older set of stages back on screen, and a backend that has stalled collects an outstanding call every two seconds for as long as it is stalled. The next read is scheduled by the previous one landing.
- [Fixed] Say which sources a long search is actually asking, and which are off. The live line handled a source that was working, one that had answered and one that could not, and dropped the ones the user had switched off, which is exactly when a deliberately narrowed search looks like a build that never had those sources. The count beside it came from the previous search's roster, so it could name five while three were being asked. Both come from the running search now, and the line is shown for a search rather than for the shared busy flag every press on that screen sets, which had been labelling a download, a Retry and a cancel as "Searching".
- [Changed] Hand the deployment the package it is about to install. A passing `release` run built the artifact and proved its exact SHA-256, and then wrote each of those into a different stage log: the install command takes the digest as a mandatory argument rather than a discovery guess, so every deployment began by reading a log file to find it. The run now prints `package:` and `sha256:` on its own summary and records them in `qa-summary.json`, and the contract points at the summary instead of at two logs.
- [Changed] Make a truncated read of the operating contract something the reader can notice. `AGENTS.md` asks to be read completely and gives no way to tell whether that happened, and a tool that cuts its output short at two kilobytes hands back an opening that looks like the whole thing. Step one now names the section the file ends with, and repository QA refuses a section appended below that one, because the instruction would otherwise be a lie told to exactly the reader who cannot see the end of the file. The complete-Linux-gate commands gained the one that proves a green run belongs to the exact head, which is the only run the contract treats as ready and which a plain listing cannot answer, with the note that it is read on request or after a fix rather than routinely.
- [Changed] Say what could not run, not only what failed. A run that fails one stage and leaves another unable to start described the failure and passed over the other in a single word, and a stage that could not run is what a collection error looks like: the backend suite went down because one module did not parse, the summary printed the frontend's tail instead, and the file that holds the rest is in a run directory the CI runner throws away. The bounded tail is now printed for the first of each, headed by the stage it belongs to and followed by that stage's own rerun command.
- [Security] Show the whole of a line the table is allowed to carry. The backend hands the code view one line of up to 2048 bytes and reports it as not cut, and the window is 26 rows of about 68 columns: paging only ever broke between whole lines, so a line wider than the window got a page of its own and everything past the 26th row was clipped by the block that draws it. No later page held the rest, nothing on screen said anything was missing, and a wide character halved the point it started disappearing at. On the one screen that exists to show exactly what an exact SHA would execute, the end of a script was simply not there. A long line is cut into rows now, at the width the block wraps at and counted in columns rather than characters, and paging is arithmetic on those, so every byte the backend hands over is reachable with Next.
- [Fixed] Apply the ceiling on a section's lines while they are made rather than after. A table inside every accepted bound can still hold a script that is millions of tiny lines, and the whole of it was split into a Python string per line before all but the first 8192 were dropped again: tens of megabytes of the backend's memory for a table the user is only reading, spent on the screen they read it on and before they have consented to anything. The lines are counted without being built and built only as far as the ceiling.
- [Fixed] Let one table give one answer about the window it carries. `<Forms>` is the container and can be present and empty, exactly as `<Files>` can, and counting the container itself as executable content meant Review and the stored metadata called such a table executable window content while **Look inside this table**, which lists the windows themselves, had nothing to show. That is the one place the two must agree, because the list is what justifies the consent asked for beside it. A window is now what the container holds, including a child tag this does not recognise, with an embedded payload excepted because it is a payload wherever a file chooses to write it, and inspection, the stored metadata and the code view all ask the same question of the same bytes.
- [Fixed] Make a value list of thousands usable with a controller. The parser's ceiling was raised to 16384 this version for a real table that declares 6508 items in each of three pickers, which made those tables parse and left them unusable: the whole list went into one Decky dropdown, which a controller walks one item at a time with no search, no paging and no way to jump, and a read-only picker is the one control with no typed value to fall back on. Past a screenful the list is narrowed by typing, the dropdown is handed a bounded number of options, and the value the record is set to stays among them so the picker cannot show a selection it does not hold. Every option offered is still one the table declares.
- [Fixed] Keep every module inside the syntax of the interpreter this plugin runs on. Decky's loader ships CPython 3.11.7 and the complete Linux gate pins 3.11 to match it, while the development device is on 3.13, and one test file used an escape inside an f-string replacement field, which is Python 3.12 syntax: the file did not parse, so the whole backend suite went with it as a gate that could not run rather than as a test that failed, and everything about it looked correct on the machine the change was written on. Repository QA now parses every tracked module at the runtime's own version, and reads the two f-string forms out of the token stream because the grammar check cannot see either of them.
- [Fixed] Stop rebuilding a whole group of actions because the press it is running disabled them. The row already latched which element wraps it, and the group of buttons inside that row still decided from how many of its controls were reachable at that instant: pressing **Refresh runtime** on Advanced's Runtime row disabled both buttons beside the `?`, took the group below the two controls that make it a group, and unmounted and mounted every button in it, once when the press started and once when it ended. The focus ring goes with them, and so does anything holding the button that was pressed. A group holds the wrapper across a press, which is the rule the row itself already follows, and only while both controls are still there: a row that has genuinely lost one goes back to a plain block rather than keeping a container Steam can land on and do nothing with.
- [Changed] Recover the last verified Decky plugin root with one bounded read-only command when the live process cannot supply it, instead of manually searching target-session records.
- [Security] Traverse every element accepted by the table parser when **Look inside this table** lists executable content. The first deep-nesting guard stopped a recursive crash by stopping at element depth 128, which also let a Lua, Auto Assembler, form or embedded-file section below that point disappear from the exact-SHA review while the inspector still classified the table as executable. The walk is iterative now, stays under the existing file and element-count bounds, and cannot hide an accepted section behind Python call depth.
- [Fixed] Keep the 0.9.14 dense heading and row rules inside all five detached workflow modals, not only the main panel and the newer code/search screens. Also keep the imported-table modal open while its async selection is committing: controller Back, Escape and the visible **Back** action now obey the same latch as the disabled **Use** rows instead of dismissing the workflow while its durable handoff continues unseen.
- [Fixed] Send a direct local `.CT` through import before archive inspection so malformed exact bytes reach the durable not-working record with the selected game, and keep RAR capability current without hiding results on a failed probe. The 7-Zip cache is keyed by the observed executable identity rather than its pathname, an in-place package update invalidates it, and only a successful format listing may say that a RAR handler is absent.
- [Fixed] Cut a long line in one pass instead of one character at a time. Trimming a value to its byte ceiling dropped the last character and re-encoded the rest to ask how long it was now, which is quadratic in a length a stranger's file chooses: measured here, one 2 MB value took 66 seconds against a 32 MB table ceiling. Every description, label and dropdown value of every imported table goes through it, before any consent and on the ordinary import route, and so does every line of every script the new code view shows. A table with one minified or generated line could hold the backend for hours. The same rule now lives in one place and slices the encoded text once: the same 2 MB value takes three milliseconds.
- [Fixed] Stop rebuilding a panel row because the press it is running disabled its own button. Almost every action here disables itself for the length of the press it started, and the row decided which element wrapped it from whether it had anything enabled on it, so pressing **Use** in the imported-table list or **Read** in the code view unmounted the button under the focus that was on it and put it back afterwards, twice per press. A row that has ever had a control is now a row with a control; one whose only action is disabled from the start, which is what a Debug row is, still gets the wrapper that makes it reachable.
- [Fixed] Say that a whole value list was dropped, not that one value was. A list past the byte ceiling is dropped whole so one record's picker cannot cost the table, and the list this was added for holds 6508 values: counted among the values, Review told a user deciding whether to use the table that it had lost one. The two are counted and said separately now.
- [Changed] Run one frontend test file on its own. `python scripts/qa.py --vitest <path-or-pattern>` is the counterpart of `--pytest`: iterating on one of the eighteen component files paid for the other seventeen every time, half a minute a run. It combines with `--pytest` into a single run, prints the same exact rerun command on a failure, and fails rather than passing empty when a pattern matches no file. It type-checks nothing, so `--profile frontend` stays the gate.
- [Fixed] Record an archive nothing here can open, so it stops being offered. Every member of it being an encrypted `.7z` or `.rar` is terminal for those exact bytes, because CE Decky will not put a password on another program's command line, and it was the one terminal outcome that wrote nothing down: the row came back on the next search, cost another provider countdown and another download, and ended in the same refusal. It was also the one mark in the search list with no entry behind it in the list the user reads and clears. It is recorded as what it is rather than as bytes that are not a table, with **Encrypted** as its own status, because the archive may well hold a good table and re-packing it as a zip is something the user can actually do.
- [Fixed] Four defects found hunting over the whole of this version. The game a table was tried on was recorded on three routes into the not-working list and not on the other eight, which are the routes where it is least in doubt: every one of them takes the AppID as a parameter, so a table that failed to be read while pinning a cheat, saving a startup plan or preparing a session joined the list with no game on it. **Look inside this table** listed every script under the groups it sits in, and those groups are the same for every script in a table whose author nests everything under two instruction steps: each row is one truncated line, so all of them read as the same 128 characters and the size and the line count behind them never appeared. That context now drops whatever every row repeats and follows the facts that tell the rows apart. An ordinary import paid for a profile read it only needed if the file turned out not to be a table. And an unreadable record is now read as unreadable however the read failed, not only when it failed at the parser.
- [Changed] Let a cheat section just say its own name. Dropping the groups every section shares left the same repetition one branch down, so a group whose parent was an option two rows up carried that parent's whole name and got cut for width: three of one table's eight options read as an ellipsis, an arrow and a leaf. A CT holds a group's descendants directly under it, so a listed parent is always the row above and goes. The author's own double spaces go with it, since a heading is not a label. Whole groups are then dropped off the front while the name is too long, in silence: what goes is either on the screen as the option this one sits under, or is a heading the picker never offers, and a mark saying so pointed at nothing while costing two characters of the name.
